Theorem 4.1

Checking projectability of Tame PSMs is in
PSPACE
.
One can also synthesize a projection in
PSPACE
.

4.2
Mixed Choice yields Undecidable Projectability

Now, we show that the sender-driven choice restriction for Tame PSMs is necessary for projectability to be decidable.
General PSMs inherit undecidability of projectability from HMSCs,
which in turn was proven by
Lohrey [59, Thm. 3.4]
.
Given our positive result for Tame PSMs,
the proof for undecidability ought to break
in the presence of sender-driven choice.
The original proof goes through several (often implicitly given or omitted)
automata-based transformations
and does not give any insights about where and how the transformations
break under the assumption of sender-driven choice.

Theorem 4.2

The projectability problem for sink-final mixed-choice Σ1-PSMs is undecidable.

We reduce the membership problem for Turing Machines to checking projectability of a sink-final mixed-choice
Σ1-PSM
with five participants. Initially, there is a branching which only two participants are involved in and learn about.
Subsequently, all participants communicate Turing machine computations in the form of configurations in both branches.
If the (projected) language of one of the other participants is not the same for both branches,
the PSM cannot be implementable because they do not know which branch to comply with and easily deadlock.
We also show that the reverse is the case.
Hence, we specify a language for each branch and make both coincide if and only if the Turing Machine has no accepting computation, which is the case if and only if the PSM is projectable.

The full proof is in

Section

0.B.3
.
We adopt the proof strategy of Lohrey to PSMs and
make every transformation explicit and carefully check
which structural properties the transformations preserve,
yielding a stronger undecidability result concerning the most rudimentary of PSMs: Σ1-PSMs.

5
Typing Programs against CSMs

We now overview the key ideas behind AMP’s type system.
The formal details and full proofs can be found in

Appendix

0.C
.
To define programs,
we take inspiration from the process calculus
with session interleaving and delegation of
[
69
]
.
The syntax of AMP’s programs is reproduced in

Fig.

10
.
The processes
P
represent the static program text.
As is standard,
\mathbf{0}
is the terminated process,
\parallel
denotes parallel composition,

\mathsf{Q}[\vec{c}]
denotes a sequential process running the code
defined by a finite set of definitions
\mathcal{D}
.
The prefixes

\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle
and

\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})

denote internal and external choice respectively,
with a non-empty finite set of indices
I
.
The endpoint of participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
of a channel between
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
in a session
s
, is denoted by
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]
;

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can send a label
l
and some payload
p
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
in session
s

by
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]!l\langle p\rangle
,
the dual reception is denoted by
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]?l\langle x\rangle

(which binds the payload to
x
).
To model delegation, a process must be able to send to another the capability
to act as participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in session
s
, denoted
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
; the receiving process will bind such capability to a variable
x
and use it to form endpoints
x[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]
; we thus have in general send/receive actions
on
c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]
where
c
can be a variable or some
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
.

The process
(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P

denotes the creation of a new bound session
s
used in
P
.
The session is annotated with a (computationally irrelevant) CSM
\mathcal{A}
,
taking the place of what is often a global type.
So far, we treated messages in CSMs very abstractly
as elements of a finite alphabet.
In processes, messages are more structured:
they have a label (from a finite set) and a payload (of some type).
The messages used by the CSM will thus be pairs
l(t)

of a label
l
and a payload type
t
, with the convention that
if, from a state
q
, there are two outgoing transitions with the same sender,
receiver and label, they will agree on the type.

In applications, the payload can be of any base type
(e.g. integers, strings),
or be a channel capability
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
(for delegation).
Since supporting base types is a simple exercise, we follow

[
69
]

and focus on the harder case of channel capabilities as payloads.
When using a CSM
\mathcal{A}=\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

as a protocol specification for a session
s
,
it is natural to consider the (control) states
Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
of
A_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

to be the local types that can be associated to
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
. Therefore, in our setting we will consider
the set
L
of the states of
any
\mathcal{A}
annotating the process, as the possible payload types.
For simplicity, we assume all CSMs use disjoint sets of states,
so that we can unambiguously refer to the transitions from any state
q

by
\delta(q)
.

In particular, if the protocol specified by
\mathcal{A}
can delegate
channels of a session following some CSM
\mathcal{B}
,
then the message alphabet of
\mathcal{A}
will include states of
\mathcal{B}
.
When the CSMs are obtained through projection, it is natural to first
obtain
\mathcal{B}
so we can refer to its states in writing the PSM that projects to
\mathcal{A}
.
We thus assume there is an acyclic “delegation partial order”
between the CSMs of a process:

\mathcal{B}<\mathcal{A}
means that
\mathcal{A}
can use the states of
\mathcal{B}
in its messages.

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\Coloneqq x\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pts[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbf{0}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptP_{1}\parallel P_{2}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\mathsf{Q}[\vec{c}]

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbf{0}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptR_{1}\parallel R_{2}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,R\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\smash{\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\smash{\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\mathsf{Q}[\vec{c}]

\displaystyle\mid\hskip 3.0pts\blacktriangleright\sigma\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\mathbf{err}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\bigl(\mathsf{Q}[\vec{x}]=\smash{\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]}!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}\bigr);\;\mathcal{D}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\bigl(\mathsf{Q}[\vec{x}]=\smash{\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]}?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}\bigr);\;\mathcal{D}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\varepsilon

Figure 10:
Syntax of AMP’s
\pi
-
calculus.

The semantics of the calculus is defined on runtime configurations
R

(defined in
Fig.

10
),
which are processes which additionally contain message queues

s\blacktriangleright\sigma
for each active session
s
.
Here
\sigma
is a map from pairs of participants to
sequences of messages.
The reduction semantics is standard (cf.
Appendix

0.C
).
The only reduction rules we highlight here are the ones leading to an error
configuration:

\small\frac{\forall i\in I.\,\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=l\langle\_\rangle\ldots\land l_{i}\neq l}{\ExtCh_{i\in I}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}\parallel s\blacktriangleright\sigma\longrightarrow\mathbf{err}}\par\frac{\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\neq\varepsilon\text{ for some }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}{(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\;s\blacktriangleright\sigma\longrightarrow\mathbf{err}}

The first rule models
unsafe communication
:
a process is stuck
because all the queues it is waiting to receive from
are not empty, but the labels of the first messages do not match any of the cases the process is expecting.
The second rule models
orphan messages
: a session where all participants terminated but that has still non-empty message queues.
The safety guarantees of our type system will rule out both cases.
Note that
[
69
,
68
]
focuses on communication safety.
In addition, they consider S-deadlock freedom, which implies no orphan messages, but is an undecidable property that needs to be checked and is not necessarily transferred to processes by the type system: the property only holds if there is only one session, in which case much stronger conditions transfer.
In our setting, deadlock freedom is transferred throughout by projection and the type system, yielding no orphan messages.

\frac{}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\mathbf{0}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\mathbf{0}$} }\par\par\frac{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{end}(q)}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}c:q,\Lambda\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-end} }\par\frac{\delta(q)=\{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?l_{i}(p_{i}),q_{i})\mid i\in I\}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\forall i\in I.\,}\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q_{i},y_{i}:p_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P_{i}}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\ExtCh$} }\par\frac{\delta(q)\supseteq\{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!l_{i}(p_{i}),q_{i})\mid i\in I\}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\forall i\in I.\,}\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q_{i},\{c_{j}:p_{j}\}_{j\in I\setminus\{i\}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P_{i}}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q,\{c_{i}:p_{i}\}_{i\in I}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\IntCh$} }\par\frac{\Lambda_{s}=\{s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:\operatorname{init}(\mathcal{A}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}})\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\!\!\mathcal{A}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,\Lambda_{s}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\upnu$} }

Figure 11:

Typing rules for processes;

\operatorname{init}(\hbox{-})
denotes a CSM’s initial state.

Figure

11
shows the crucial rules of AMP’s type system.
The typing judgement

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P

uses
a process
P
​,
a typing context
\Theta

for the types of the parameters
\vec{c}
of
sequential processes
\mathsf{Q}[\vec{c}]

(the definitions of which are typed separately against
\Theta
);
a typing context
\Lambda

associating the variables
x

and the channel capabilities
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]

occurring free in
P
, with some CSM state
q\in L
.
Rule
PT-
\mathbf{0}
says that a terminated process
is typable in the environment with no capabilities.
Rule
PT-end
permits to discard the capabilities that have terminated:

\operatorname{end}(q)
holds for final states with no outgoing receive transition.
Rules
PT-
\ExtCh
and
PT-
\IntCh

deal with communication.
Assume
c=s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
.
According to
PT-
\ExtCh
,
to receive a message as participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in session
c
,
we look for the type
q
of
c
in the typing context and check the CSM transitions
\delta(q)
are all receives
\{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?l_{i}(p_{i}),q_{i})\mid i\in I\}
.
Then the process needs to be able to receive any branch
i
,
resulting in the continuation
P_{i}
which is typed in the context
extended with the corresponding payload type
y_{i}:p_{i}
,
and with the type of
c
changed to
q_{i}
.
According to
PT-
\IntCh
,
to send, as
c
with type
q
, a message non-deterministically picked from a number of branches
i\in I
, we have to make sure
q
allows each branch,
including matching the types of the payloads.
Then each branch
i
continues as
P_{i}
which is typed in a context
where
c
has type
q_{i}
and we lost ownership of the payload
c_{i}
.
Finally,
PT-
\upnu
types a new session
s
used by
P
,
by adding to the context a new binding
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for each
participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
of the CSM
\mathcal{A}
annotating the session,
with
q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
being the initial state of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
\mathcal{A}
.

The first correctness criterion for the type system is to prove
subject reduction: if a process is typable, then every configuration reachable from it will be typable.
Thus, to state subject reduction,
we need to define when a runtime configuration is typable.
For this purpose, we define a second judgement

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;R

that includes a third typing context
\Omega

used to type session message queues:
associating to each channel
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]
a sequence of message types

l(q)
(label and payload type).
The key to make typing of runtime configurations an inductive invariant,
is the following rule:

\small\frac{(\vec{q},\xi)\in\operatorname{reach}(\mathcal{A})\hskip 9.24994pt\Lambda^{s}_{\vec{q}}=\{s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:\vec{q}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\mathcal{A}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Omega^{s}_{\xi}=\{s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]::\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\mathcal{A}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,\Lambda^{s}_{\vec{q}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega,\Omega^{s}_{\xi}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;R}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,R}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={RT-$\upnu$} }

The main difference between
RT-
\upnu
and
PT-
\upnu
is
that the typing context is not populated with capabilities
associated to initial states; instead the prover can pick any CSM
configuration
(\vec{q},\xi)

—where
\vec{q}
collects the local state of each participant, and
\xi
the contents of the message queues—
that is reachable from the initial configuration of
\mathcal{A}
.
The states and the queues are used to initialise the typing context
to type the process
R
using the restricted session.

In what follows we assume
the definitions
\mathcal{D}
can be typed according to
\Theta
.
We say a process/runtime configuration is
well-annotated
if
every CSM appearing in it is

(i)

deadlock-free, and

(ii)

satisfies feasible eventual reception.

Here,
annotated
indicates that the CSMs have no computational meaning but
well
shows the need for certain guarantees, which our type system can preserve.
Note that a process is automatically well-annotated
if the CSMs are obtained via projection.

Theorem 5.1
(Subject Reduction)

Given a well-annotated
R
,
if
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;R
and
R\longrightarrow R^{\prime}
,
then

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;R^{\prime}.

Corollary 1
(Type Safety)

For a well-annotated
R
,
if
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;R
and
R\longrightarrow^{*}R^{\prime}
,
then
R^{\prime}
does not contain
\mathbf{err}
.

For progress, the situation is more delicate:
just like in
[
69
]
and most other MST systems, allowing session interleaving may introduce inter-session dependencies that are not modelled in the global protocol (which only pertains intra-session dependencies).
We thus prove progress under these assumptions:

(i)

there is only one session running, and

(ii)

that each of its participants is implemented by a distinct process, and

(iii)

the CSM annotating it is sink-final.

To encode these extra restrictions, we define a “Session Fidelity” variant of the typing judgement

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash_{\tiny{SF}}}\;R

which uses a subset of the rules of
\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;
to enforce the restrictions above.
Let
\Lambda^{s}_{\vec{q}}
and
\Omega^{s}_{\xi}
be
defined as in the premises of
RT-
\upnu
.

Theorem 5.2
(Progress)

Let
(\vec{q},\xi)
be a configuration of a
sink-final, deadlock-free
CSM
\mathcal{A}
satisfying feasible eventual reception.
If
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda^{s}_{\vec{q}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega^{s}_{\xi}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash_{\tiny{SF}}}\;R
,
and
(\vec{q},\xi)
can take a step,
then there exist some
R^{\prime}
and
(\vec{q}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
,
such that

R\longrightarrow R^{\prime}
,

(\vec{q},\xi)\longrightarrow(\vec{q}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
,
and

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda^{s}_{\vec{q}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega^{s}_{\xi^{\prime}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash_{\tiny{SF}}}\;R^{\prime}.

Progress hinges on deadlock freedom of the CSM.
In general, any (language) property of a PSM that is preserved and reflected by
\mathcal{C}(\hbox{-})
holds for its projection.
However, as for progress, it is not necessarily easy to make the type system enforce the preservation of these properties at the global process level and requires careful treatment.

[
25
]
demonstrated how Kobayashi-style techniques
[
52
]

that can be used to show progress in the presence of session interleaving.
We conjecture a similar system can be
added on top of AMP’s type system.

6
Applications of AMP to MST Frameworks

Standard (expression-based) global types from MST frameworks
can be seen as restricted special cases of PSMs.
What is gained from using AMP for global types seen as PSMs?
Is anything lost in doing so?
In this section, we evaluate AMP as a backend for projection/typing of
standard global types.
The key consequences of our results are:

(a)

Every sender-driven global type
is a tame sink-final
Σ1-PSM
.

(b)

Tame sink-final Σ1-PSMs can be represented as a
sender-driven global type.

(c)

Every collection of (expression-based) local types

\{\!\!\{L_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

can be expressed as a CSM

\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

and vice versa.

(d)

AMP’s projection is deadlock-free by construction,
but MSTs typically insist on freedom of a stricter notion of deadlock
which we call
soft deadlock
.
We show AMP’s projection can also be set
to ensure soft deadlock freedom, without losing completeness.

These results help us settle two open questions:

•

Expression-based global/local types are equi-expressive with respect to
state-machine-based global/local specifications.

•

Allowing mixed-choice in global types makes
projectability undecidable.

Here, we give an overview while

Appendix

0.D
presents the results in detail.

Global and local types.

In most MST frameworks,
protocols are specified using expression-based global types (
G
),
which get projected to expression-based local types (
L
).
Their syntax is specified as follows:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbf{0}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993ptm_{i}\,.\,G_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptμX\,.\,G\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptX\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(global types)}

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbf{0}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}\,.\,L_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\ExtCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}\,.\,L_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptμX\,.\,L\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptX\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(local types)}

where

\mathbf{0}
explicitly denotes termination and

μX
binds a recursion variable
X
. The remaining operators specify how messages are exchanged:
for local types, sending and receiving are separate actions,
while for global types they are specified in a single paired event.
Typically only
deterministic
global types are considered,
i.e. where
every
\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{i}\,.\,G_{i}

has no
i\neq j
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{j}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{j}
.
The choice restrictions we discussed, can be imposed on global types,
e.g. sender-driven choice requires that, for

\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{i}\,.\,G_{i}
,
for all
i,j\in I
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{j}
.

The standard semantics of global types has been given as a transition system,
or as sets of traces.
In both cases,
the semantics allows reordering of events that are not causally related,
e.g.
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt1\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt2\,.\,\mathbf{0}

allows
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
to communicate before
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
This is formalised, in the presentation of
[
60
,
70
,
57
]

(which we adopt here)
as defining the semantics of a global type to be a set of traces
closed under the indistinguishability relation
\sim
.
With this view, it is immediate to represent any global type as a PSM.
Given the restricted format of global types,
the PSMs corresponding to translations of global types
(like the one in
Fig.

4
)
are Σ1-PSMs with a specific shape:
they are tree-like, sink-final and recursion only happens at leaves and to ancestors
[
70
]
.
On the face of it,
it is unclear whether every
Σ1-PSM
can be modelled as global type.

Theorem 6.1

For every sink-final
Σ1-PSM
M, there is a global type
\mathsf{GAut}(M)
with the same core language (and hence the same semantics).
If M is non-deterministic (mixed-choice, sender-driven, or directed, resp.), then
\mathsf{GAut}(M)
is non-deterministic (mixed-choice, sender-driven, or directed, resp.).

The main idea of
Theorem

6.1
is that one can
see a global type as a special regular expression, and thus
we can adapt techniques like Arden’s lemma and Brzozowski derivatives
to the case of PSMs.
The key difficulty in the proof
lays in showing the branching conditions are preserved:
the standard automata transformations change the branching structure,
and we need to produce new variants that do.

Similarly, local types can be directly read as the FSMs of a CSM.
We can also provide an inverse transformation (preserving branching).

Theorem 6.2

Let
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a sink-final FSM over
\Gamma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
without mixed-choice states
for a participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
One can construct a local type
L_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
with
\mathcal{L}(L_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=\mathcal{L}(A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.

Deadlocks and protocol termination.

In MSTs, local types can only terminate with a
\mathbf{0}
,
which signals at the same time that it is valid to stop the protocol,
and that there is no further potential action.
This implies that for a global type to be projectable into local types,
all the participants need to know unambiguously when the protocol terminated globally.
In contrast, using CSMs,
it is possible to mark as final a state with outgoing transitions.
Consider for instance the (directed) global type

\small G\coloneq({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993ptm_{1}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993ptm_{1}\,.\,\mathbf{0})+({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993ptm_{2}\,.\,\mathbf{0})
.
AMP’s projection would produce the FSM

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m_{1}

as the projection for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
It contains a non-sink final state:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is not informed of which branch was taken and needs to be prepared
to terminate, or receive one more message.

AMP’s projection produces deadlock-free CSMs, where deadlocks are defined
as configurations which cannot take a step, but
their queues are not empty or some participant is in a non-final state.
Projections to local types ensure the absence of another type of deadlock:
a
soft deadlock
, i.e. a configuration that is a deadlock, or that
cannot take a step but where some participant is in a
non-sink
state.
Is the possibility of soft deadlocks desirable?
We argue that this depends on the domain of application:
in distributed computing, it would be fine if a server kept listening for incoming requests while, in embedded computing, it can be key that all participants eventually stop.
We can show that it is possible to use AMP in both scenarios, without giving up on completeness.

Definition 12
(Strong Projectability)

A language
L\subseteq\Gamma^{\omega}
is said to be
strongly projectable
if there exists a CSM
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that

\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is free from soft deadlocks

(soft deadlock freedom)
,
and

L
is the language of
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

(protocol fidelity)
.
We say that
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a strong projection of
L
.

Theorem 6.3

Let
G
be a projectable global type.
Then, the subset construction
\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})

[
57
, Def. 5.4]
is sink-final for every participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
if and only if there is a CSM that is a strong projection of
G
and this CSM satisfies feasible eventual reception or every of its state machines is deterministic.

If we aim for a strong projection of a projectable global type, we construct the global type’s subset construction and check if it is sink-final.
If it is not, there is no strong projection of it.
If this is undesirable, the protocol needs redesigning.

Theorem

6.2
can yield local types and

\mathsf{LAut}(L)
is the FSM for a local type
L
.

Undecidability for mixed-choice.

Finally, these results together with our results from

Section

4.2
,
can settle the open question of whether we can project
mixed-choice global types algorithmically.

Corollary 2

Both the projectability problem and the strong projectability problem for mixed-choice global types are undecidable.

7
Related Work

Multiparty session types.

Inspired by linear logic
[
40
]
,

Honda [43]
proposed binary sessions types
for sessions with two participants.
Multiparty session types
[
44
]
extended the idea to multiple participants.

Deniélou and Yoshida [30]

were the first to extensively explore the relation between CSMs and local types, but their projection is not complete and only supports directed choice;
moreover the approach was found to be somewhat flawed
[
69
]
.
MSTs have been incorporated into a number of programming languages

[
5
,
50
,
58
,
55
,
67
,
64
,
24
]
.
They have also been applied to various other domains like
operating systems
[
32
]
,
web services
[
80
]
,
distributed algorithms
[
53
]
,
timed systems
[
12
]
,
cyber-physical systems
[
61
]
, and
smart contracts
[
29
]
.
A number of works are devoted to mechanising MST meta-theory
[
73
,
21
,
49
,
48
]
.
Our results could potentially extend the expressivity of the types involved in these applications.

MST projectability/projection.

Via a reduction to globally-cooperative HMSCs,
Stutz [70]
proved MST projectability to be decidable for the class of global types that can —but do not have to— terminate (called
\mathbf{0}
-reachable).

Li et al. [57]
provided a direct MST projection algorithm that is complete for sender-driven global types, providing a PSPACE upper bound. Our results use a reduction to these later developments.
The global specifications in
[
57
]

can be shown to be special cases of Tame Σ1-PSMs so our results strictly expand the reach of their results.
For example, the protocols of

Figs.

5
and
7

are all tame, yet out of the reach of both works.
We also clarify
the discrepancy between the notion of deadlock in global types and in

[
57
]

(cf.
Section

6
).
Finally,
[
70
,
57
]

do not have a type system, providing no way to link properties of projections with the ones of processes.
Preliminary versions of some of our results
appeared in the PhD thesis of the first author
[
71
]
.

The almost totality of asynchronous MST works can only handle directed choice.
An exception is
[
19
]
,
where unrestricted global types are considered (without a type system).
They propose an incomplete projection algorithm that is correct with respect to
a different notion of correct projection than the standard one we adopt and generalise.
We refer to
[
60
]
for a survey on choice restrictions.

Hu and Yoshida [46]
propose a scheme with global types
and an incomplete projection,
where the global types are not safe by construction
and the restrictions on choice only appear at the local types.
The safety of global types is ensured by a combination of model-checking
with message buffers of size 1,
and syntactic restrictions that ensure that any unsafety
that might arise, will be visible in the 1-bounded executions.
For PSMs satisfying the syntactic restrictions,
the same approach could be applied.
The types of
[
46
]
also include connect/disconnect actions,
which can be emulated in AMP by excluding deadlocks (but not soft deadlocks)
and using non-sink final states.

Choreography automata and languages.

Choreography Automata
[
7
]
are syntactically similar to Σ1-PSMs,
but do not employ any closure operation, requiring the user to specify all the allowed interleavings, and preventing finite state representations for many common communication patterns.
In addition,
Majumdar et al. [60]
showed that their conditions for projectability are flawed for the asynchronous case
(fixes for the synchronous case appeared in
[
38
, p. 8]
).

Barbanera et al. [8]
applies a language-theoretic
approach to a limited class of
synchronous
choreographies
(with no claim of completeness of projection).

Bottom-up MSTs.

A number of MST-based works deviate from the top-down approach.
For instance,
[
69
]

proposes a type system that only requires local types and not a global type.
The typing ensures some operational correspondence between local types and processes, making it possible to model check local types to determine properties of the program.
Their local types in the asynchronous setting are Turing-powerful, and therefore model checking is of limited use.
By virtue of the decoupling achieved by our type system,
AMP can be used in a bottom-up way too:
safety of the CSM used to type a process, implies safety of the process,
regardless of whether the CSM is obtained by projection or just given.

Dagnino et al. [28]
and
Castellani et al. [20]
also use a bottom-up strategy
by reconstructing
a so-called
deconfined
global type
from the parallel composition of local programs
of a single session.
Deconfined global types are not automatically safe,
and checking their safety is shown to be undecidable.

Extensions for MSTs.

A number of extensions for MSTs have been considered
(see
[
11
]
for a survey),
including:
parametrisation
[
23
,
31
]
,
dependent types
[
75
,
31
,
76
]
,
graduality
[
47
]
,
fault-tolerance
[
79
,
10
]
.
Context-free session types
[
74
,
51
]
specify binary sessions that are not representable with finite-control.
It would be interesting to consider projection for PSMs generated by pushdown automata.

Distributed synthesis.

In automata theory,
distributed synthesis seeks a way to transform a sequential specification into an equivalent distributed implementation, which is close in spirit
with the idea of extracting local types from global types.
One of the few positive results in this area is Zielonka’s theorem
[
81
]
,
which shows that every regular trace language can be recognised by a so-called “asynchronous automaton”.
Despite their name, asynchronous automata can be seen as a parallel composition
of participants interacting through
synchronous
actions.
In contrast, PSMs and CSMs represent non-regular FIFO languages,
giving rise to a harder challenge.

High-level message sequence charts.

HMSCs were defined in an industry standard
[
78
]
,
inspiring extensive academic research
[
62
,
36
,
35
,
33
,
66
]
.
Projectability has been studied for HMSCs under the name “safe realisability”

[
37
,
59
,
3
]
,
and was shown to be undecidable in general
[
59
]
.
Several restrictions of HMSCs have been proposed to make projectability decidable.
For a detailed survey, we refer to
[
70
]
.
Compared to PSMs, HMSCs only model finite runs; their PSM encoding equips them with an infinite run semantics.
With our developments, it is fairly straightforward to obtain a projection operation for sender-driven, sink-final
HMSCs
that respect some channel bounds.
This class is incomparable to any of the decidable HMSC classes proposed in the literature.
Since our type system only depends on CSMs, regardless of how they are obtained,
AMP can type check a program against a projectable HMSC.

Choreographic programming.

Choreographic programming
[
26
,
39
,
42
]
adopts the top-down approach even more radically than MSTs.
In choreographic programming, the endpoint projection (EPP) aims at synthesizing a fully-featured program implementation directly from the global specification.
As a result, the global specification describes the local computation alongside the communication structure (requiring infinite-state formalisms).
In choreographies, one typically works with non-finite-control-state global specifications,
so the hopes for a complete and decidable EPP are slim, justifying giving up on completeness. By only considering local computation in processes, the MST/AMP approach avoids this issue.
Nevertheless, our results
could still be useful for EPP
when applied to the pure communication structure of choreographies.
Notably, our method can project examples that cannot be projected using EPPs from the literature.
Consider the choreography

\textbf{if }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.{\star}\textbf{ then }({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\_\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\_)\textbf{ else }({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\_\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\_)
,
where message payloads are irrelevant and hence omitted
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.{\star}
denotes non-deterministic choice by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
The example is syntactically valid in
[
27
]

and can be easily encoded as a global type with sender-driven choice.
However, their EPP
would be undefined for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
:
it
uses the merge from

[
18
]
,
which can only merge same sender receives. Our results would instead produce the desired projection.

Communicating state machines.

CSMs are the canonical automata model for distributed systems.
They have been studied in the context of model checking projections and do not apply a top-down methodology.
The verification problem is undecidable in general since CSMs are Turing-powerful
[
14
]
.
Several strategies to yield decidable classes have been proposed:
assuming channels are lossy
[
1
]
, restricting the communication topology
[
65
,
77
]
, or
only allowing half-duplex communication for two participants
[
22
]
.
The concept of existential boundedness
[
34
]
was initially defined for CSMs and yields decidability of control state reachability. The same holds for synchronisability
[
13
,
41
]
, which, intuitively, requires that every execution can be re-ordered (up to
\sim
) into phases of sends and receives such that messages can only be received in the same phase.
Global types can only express
1
-synchronisable and half-duplex communication
[
72
]
.

Appendix

Appendix 0.A
Additional Material for
Section

3

0.A.1
Communicating State Machines

Definition 13
(Semantics of Communicating State Machines)

We denote the set of channels with
\mathsf{Chan}=\{\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\}
.
The set of global states of a CSM is given by
\prod_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Given a global state
q
,
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
denotes the state of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
q
.
A
configuration
of a CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a pair
(q,\xi)
, where
q
is a global state and

\xi:\mathsf{Chan}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
is a mapping of each channel to its current content.
The initial configuration
(q_{0},\xi_{\varepsilon})
consists of a global state
q_{0}
where the state of each role is the initial state
q_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
of
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and a mapping
\xi_{\varepsilon}
, which maps each channel to the empty word
\varepsilon
.
A configuration
(q,\xi)
is said to be
final
iff each individual local state
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is final for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\xi
is
\xi_{\varepsilon}
.

The global transition relation
\rightarrow
is defined as follows:

•

(q,\xi)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}(q^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m,q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\xi^{\prime}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=\xi(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)\cdot m
and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

•

(q,\xi)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m}(q^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m,q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,

q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,

\xi(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=m\cdot\xi^{\prime}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)

and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

•

(q,\xi)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}(q^{\prime},\xi)
if

(q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\varepsilon,q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for some role

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and

q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}=q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

A run of the CSM always starts with an initial configuration
(q_{0},\xi_{0})
, and is a finite or infinite sequence

(q_{0},\xi_{0})\xrightarrow{w_{0}}(q_{1},\xi_{1})\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\ldots

for which
(q_{i},\xi_{i})\xrightarrow{w_{i}}(q_{i+1},\xi_{i+1})
.
The word
w_{0}w_{1}\ldots\in\Sigma^{\infty}
is said to be the
trace
of the run.
A run is called maximal if it is infinite or finite and ends in a final configuration. As before, the trace of a maximal run is maximal.
The language
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
of the CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
consists of its set of maximal traces.
A configuration is a deadlock if it is not final and has no outgoing transitions.

Majumdar et al. [60, Lm. 22]
showed that the semantics of CSMs are closed under the indistinguishability relation
\sim
.

Lemma 2

Let
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a CSM.
Then,
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))
.

0.A.2
Feasible Eventual Reception is Preserved and Reflected by the Indistinguishability Closure

Lemma 3
(Feasible eventual reception is preserved and reflected by
\mathcal{C}(\hbox{-})
)

Let
L\subseteq\Gamma^{\infty}
be a language.
It holds that
L
satisfies feasible eventual reception
iff

\mathcal{C}(L)
satisfies feasible eventual reception.

Proof

Let us recall the definition of feasible eventual reception:
for every finite word
w\coloneq w_{1}\ldots w_{n}\in\operatorname{pref}(L)
such that
w_{i}
is an unmatched send event, there is an extension
w\leq w^{\prime}\in L
of
w
such that
w_{i}
is matched in
w^{\prime}
.

For the direction from left to right, it suffices to observe the following.
For every (finite) prefix
u\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{C}(L))
with an unmatched send event, there is
w\in L
with
u\sim w
and, by assumption, there is
w\leq w^{\prime}
where this send event is matched.
For finite words,
\sim
does not affect matching (as it only considers the send and receive events for one channel).
It is obvious that we can also use
w^{\prime}
as witness for
u
.

Let us consider the direction from right to left.
We need to show that for every finite word
w\coloneq w_{1}\ldots w_{n}\in\operatorname{pref}(L)
such that
w_{i}
is an unmatched send event, there is an extension
w\leq w^{\prime}\in L
of
w
such that
w_{i}
is matched in
w^{\prime}
.
By assumption, we know there is
u^{\prime}=w_{1}\ldots w_{n}\ldots\in\mathcal{C}(L)
with
w\leq u^{\prime}
where
w_{i}
is matched.
We do a case distinction whether
u^{\prime}
is finite.
If
u^{\prime}
is finite, there is
u\in L
with
u\sim u^{\prime}
by definition.
It is straightforward that
w_{i}
is also matched in
u
, proving the claim.
Let us suppose that
u^{\prime}
is infinite.
Despite, by definition, there is
j
such that
w_{j}
is the matching receive event for
w_{i}
.
(It is obvious that
j>n
.)
By definition of
\mathcal{C}(\hbox{-})
, there is
u\in L
with
u^{\prime}\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}u
.
This means for each finite prefix
v^{\prime}\negthinspace
of
u^{\prime}
, there is a finite prefix
v
of
u
such that

v^{\prime}\cdot v^{\prime\prime}\sim v
for some
v^{\prime\prime}
.
We choose
v^{\prime}=w_{1}\ldots w_{n}\ldots w_{j}
.
Hence, we can obtain
v\in\operatorname{pref}(L)
where
w_{j}
occurs and matches
w_{i}
because, as argued before,
\sim
does not affect matching for finite words.
For the definition of feasible eventual reception, we need a word in
L
.
We choose
u
, which is a continuation of
v
, where
w_{j}
is still matched.

\square

Appendix 0.B
Additional Material for
Section

4

Without loss of generality,
in what follows we assume there are no
\varepsilon
-transitions in a PSM
M
if
\mathcal{L}(M)=\{\varepsilon\}
.

0.B.1
Finding Channel Bounds for PSMs

Our algorithm to generate channel bounds consists of three phases.

First, we detect all channels for which we need a channel bound.
For this, we consider every transition with label

(q_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m,q_{2})

and check if there is only a single transition from
q_{2}
such that

(q_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m,q_{3})
.
If not, the channel
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle
needs to have a channel bound.
We call these
detected
channels.

Second, we check if the channel bound can be upperbounded.
For every detected channel
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle
, we consider every loop with a message from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and check the following.
We check if there is a sequence of messages from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
back to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
(possibly involving other intermediary participants),
i.e., does the loop have a subword of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}_{1}!m_{1}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m_{1}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}_{2}!m_{2}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}_{2}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}_{1}?m_{2}\cdot\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}_{n}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m_{n}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}_{n}?m_{n}\text{ for }n\geq 0.

where subword is defined in the expected way.

Intuitively, the condition enforces that any message sent in one iteration from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
needs to be received before a message from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
will be sent in the next iteration of the loop.
Hence, if satisfied,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
will not be able to go ahead with loop executions without
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
receiving its messages.
If this condition is violated for any detected channel, the PSM will not respect any channel bounds.
This condition is inspired from bounded HMSCs
[
4
,
63
]
, where they require the communication topology of every loop to form a strongly connected component, yielding that all channels will always be bounded.

Third, we compute the channel bounds.
In the second step, we ensured that loops will not add unmatched messages to the channels.
Hence, we do not need to account for loops in the computation of channel bounds.
We consider every loop-free path from the initial state.
For every detected channel, we compute the number of messages in the channel and the maximum over all loop-free paths yields the channel bound.
We shall not restrict ourselves to paths ending in final states as we also consider infinite executions and there might be loop-free paths for which no extension to a final state exists.

0.B.2
Projection Synthesis and Projectability for a Class of PSMs

Li et al. [57]
propose a sound and complete algorithm to generate projections of global types from MSTs.
Transferred to our setting, this amounts to sink-final sender-driven Σ1-PSMs.
We propose an encoding that encodes PSMs as
Σ1-PSMs.

This encoding preserves choice restrictions and is sink-final if and only if the original PSM is.

With
Example

3
,
we gave intuition about the channel participant encoding for
the KLE example.

Fig.

12
provides the respective projection and decoded FSM for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{e}}}}
.
