\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}?b_{1,1}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}!b_{1,1}\cdot\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}?b_{1,j_{1}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}!b_{1,j_{1}})\cdot\phantom{s}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}?b_{k_{1},1}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}!b_{k_{1},1}\cdot\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}?b_{k_{1},j_{k_{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}!b_{k_{1},j_{k_{1}}})

\displaystyle\text{for some }k_{1},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k_{1}}

(The projections for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{4}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{5}
are analogous to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}
and analogous reasoning applies.)
By the fact that
w
is finite and complete, we know that
k_{1}=k_{2}=k_{3}
and

i_{l}=j_{l}
for every
1\leq l\leq k
.
By the fact that
w
is FIFO-compliant, the letters coincide, i.e.
a_{i,l}=b_{i,l}

for every
i
and
l
.
Therefore, it is straightforward that
w
can be obtained by reordering

w(C_{1},D_{1},\ldots,C_{k},D_{k})

using
\sim

and, thus,
w\in L_{l}
.

End Proof of Claim 2.

Claim 3:

If
\mathit{TM}
has no accepting computation for
w
, then
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathit{TM}})
is projectable.

Proof of Claim 3.

Recall that

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathit{TM}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{3}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\mathsf{l}\cdot w\mid w\in L^{\prime}_{l}\}\uplus\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{3}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\mathsf{r}\cdot w\mid w\in L^{\prime}_{r}\}\kern 5.0pt.

If
w
is not accepted, then
L_{l}
and
L_{r}
and, hence,
L^{\prime}_{l}
and
L^{\prime}_{r}
coincide by construction.
Thus, it is irrelevant for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{4}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{5}
which branch was taken.
By Claim 2,
L_{l}
is projectable and so is
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathit{TM}})
.

End Proof of Claim 3.

Claim 4:

If
\mathit{TM}
has an accepting computation for
w
, then
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathit{TM}})
is not projectable.

Proof of Claim 4.

Let
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m})
be an accepting computation for
w
.
Then, there is

w_{u}=w(u_{1},u_{1},\ldots,u_{m},u_{m})

and, by construction, it holds that

w_{u}\notin L_{r}
.
By definition of
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathit{TM}})
, it holds that

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{2}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{3}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\mathsf{r}\cdot w_{u}\cdot w_{\mathit{end}}\notin\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathit{TM}})
.
However, for every participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,
there is

v\in\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathit{TM}})

such that

w{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=v{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Together, this contradicts closure condition
CC_{2}
which is a necessary condition for projectability.
Thus,
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathit{TM}})
is not projectable.

End Proof of Claim 4.

This concludes the proofs for all claims and hence the overall proof.

\square

Appendix 0.C
Additional Material for
Section

5

In this section, we show CSMs are a good fit for type checking.
They can be seamlessly integrated into a type system.
To show this, we present a session type system that uses CSMs as interface for type checking, instead of local types.
This clean separation of concerns makes our type system applicable to any type of protocol specification that can be projected to CSMs.
CSMs are strictly more expressive than local types.
Thus, the use of CSMs as intermediate interface improves generality without loosing efficiency.
For instance, with our projection result, we can take (Tame) PSMs as global protocol specifications and type check processes against them, with CSMs as intermediate interface for local specifications.

For the design of our type system, we follow

[
69
]

as a particularly streamlined instance of a session type system.
There are two main differences.

Scalas and Yoshida [69]

do not consider global specifications, so they do not check against a global protocol specification, but they still use local types.
In our type system, we use CSMs and show which of their properties will entail which properties of the typed program.

0.C.1
Payload Types and Delegation

In contrast to the explanation in the main text, we use global types (cf.
Definition

33
) as representations for protocols in this section to elaborate more on the payload types and delegation.
We will show that every global type can be represented as PSM but, here, their syntactic nature makes examples more concise.
While these are global specifications, all our examples transfer to CSMs but using them would be a bit harder to grasp.

So far, we treated message payloads as uninterpreted names from a fixed finite set.
In practice, each message would be a label and a payload type.
The label can indicate the branch that was taken while the payload type is interpreted as type of the data transmitted.
For instance, consider the following global type:

G\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptl(\texttt{str})+{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptr(\texttt{int})

Here,
l
and
r
are labels so
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
knows which branch was taken.
For the right branch,
int
is interpreted as type of the payload.
Thus, the payload should be of type integer, e.g. the number
2
.
If there is no payload, we simply omit it and only write the label.

A global type specifies the intended behaviour for one session.
With a type system, it is interesting to consider systems with multiple sessions that possibly follow protocols given by different global types.
For session
s
, the endpoint of participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is denoted by
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
.

Let us give an informal example of a process that follows the protocol specified by
G
; more precisely let
s
be that session.
Then, the process

P_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\parallel P_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, where
\parallel
is parallel composition,
would comply with the above global type:

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]!l\langle\text{``foo''}\rangle\,.\,0\,\IntCh\,s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]!r\langle 2\rangle\,.\,0

\displaystyle P_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]?l(x)\,.\,0\,\ExtCh\,s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]?r(y)\,.\,0

We use internal choice
\IntCh
for

P_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
because it sends first:

s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]!r\langle 2\rangle

indicates that endpoint
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
sends message
r\langle 2\rangle
to
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]
.
For
P_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, we use external choice
\ExtCh
as it cannot actually choose but receives the sent message.
It stores the payload in a variable which could be used subsequently, e.g.
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]?l(x)

stores the message in
x
.
Both processes terminate after their only action.

If all payload types are base types like
str
,
int
,
Bool
, etc., type checking is rather simple.
It gets more complicated and interesting if one adds the possibility to send channel endpoints.
This amounts to sending an endpoint of a session, e.g.
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
.
Upon receiving, the receiver shall subsequently comply with what is specified.
This is why sending a channel endpoint is called
delegation
.
Usually, local types are used to specify channel endpoint types.
However, we do not use local types so how do we specify such behaviour?
We use the states from the subset projections of a global type because this specifies the behaviour of a participant in our setting.
We assume that they are distinct across all considered state machines.
Our model uses FSMs, giving us a finite set of labels.
Note that one cannot only send the initial state.
Each state corresponds to a position in the local behaviour.
Sending non-initial states corresponds to sending subexpressions of local types.

q_{0}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
q_{4}
q_{5}
q_{6}
q_{7}
q_{8}
q_{9}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}?\textit{query}(\texttt{str})
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{price}(\texttt{int})
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\textit{buy}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}?\textit{no}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}?\textit{card}(\texttt{str})
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{valid}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{confirm}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{invalid}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{cancel}

Figure 13
:
Projection of the one buyer protocol onto seller
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
.

Example 5
(Delegation)

We consider a protocol where (one) buyer
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}
buys a book from a seller
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
who delegates the payment to a payment service
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
The one buyer protocol is specified as follows:

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{query}(\texttt{str})\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{price}(\texttt{int})\,.\,+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{buy}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{card}(\texttt{str})\,.\,G^{\prime}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{no}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}\text{ where}

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneq+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{valid}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{confirm}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{invalid}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993pt\textit{cancel}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

We project
G_{1}
onto
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
to obtain the state machine in
Fig.

13
with its set of states
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{9}\}
.
We define the second global type, which specifies the interaction between the seller
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
and the payment service
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, including delegation.

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneq+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\textit{price}(\texttt{int})\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\textit{deleg}(q_{3})\,.\,+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\textit{valid}(q_{5})\,.\,\mathbf{0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\textit{invalid}(q_{7})\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993pt\textit{no}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

First, the seller delegates checking the card details to the payment service by sending
q_{3}
.
The payment service then takes care of the payment but we do not specify this here.
Afterwards, the payment service delegates control back to the seller:
depending on the outcome of the credit card check, they send
q_{5}
or
q_{7}
.
Starting from there, the seller will either confirm or cancel.
This choice is not up to the seller but determined by the label,
valid
or
invalid
, sent by the payment service earlier.

We use the states of a projection for seller
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
as syntactic marker for the behaviour that is expected from the receiver of that channel endpoint.
Usually, this is achieved using local types.
In general, local types are less expressive than state machines.
However, with

Theorem

6.2
,
we will show that their expressivity coincides for sink-final state machines, i.e. the ones where final states have no outgoing transitions.
Hence, any sink-final state machine can be turned into a local type.
Such a local type, however, would correspond to the initial state and, with delegation, we can also send non-initial states, e.g.
q_{3}
.
Despite, it appears feasible to first construct a state machine that represents the same behaviour as starting from a non-initial state and, then, the same techniques for constructing a local type apply.
For instance, the behaviour specified by
q_{3}
can also be represented as local type:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}?\textit{card}(\texttt{str})\,.\,\IntCh\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{valid}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{confirm}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{invalid}\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}!\textit{cancel}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

We have seen an example for delegation.
There, in order to talk about states from a projection of
G_{1}
in
G_{2}
, we need access to the projection of
G_{1}
already before defining
G_{2}
.
If one considers CSMs, this is not necessarily the case.
However, we still need a similar condition to prove that well-typed processes do not leave messages in channels behind, so-called orphan messages.
Solely for this property, we assume a strict partial order
<
,
i.e. it is irreflexive, antisymmetric, and transitive, for the CSMs under consideration.
With its acyclicity, this relation provides means to decide which CSMs can use the control states of which in a system:
for every
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in a system,
if
\mathcal{A}<\mathcal{B}
, then
\mathcal{B}
can use states from
\mathcal{A}
.
We expect that this condition could be worked around with more sophisticated techniques but leave this for future work.
Interestingly,

Scalas and Yoshida [69]
,
allow delegation using local types and do not impose such restrictions.
However, they also do not prove the absence of orphan messages.
It is unclear whether their type system can be extended to prove the absence of orphan messages without such restrictions.

0.C.2
Process Calculus

We first define processes and runtime configurations.

Definition 22

Processes, runtime configurations and process definitions are defined by the following grammar:

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\Coloneqq x|s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbf{0}|P_{1}\parallel P_{2}|(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P|\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}|\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}|\mathsf{Q}[\vec{c}]

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbf{0}|R_{1}\parallel R_{2}|(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,R|\smash{\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}}|\smash{\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}}|\mathsf{Q}[\vec{c}]

\displaystyle|s\blacktriangleright\sigma|\mathbf{err}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\bigl(\mathsf{Q}[\vec{x}]=\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}\bigr);\;\mathcal{D}|\bigl(\mathsf{Q}[\vec{x}]=\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}\bigr);\;\mathcal{D}|\varepsilon

The term
c
can either be a variable
x
or a session endpoint of shape
s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]
(which will have type
q
for some state
q
).
Let us explain the constructors for processes and runtime configurations in more detail.
The term
\mathbf{0}
denotes termination while

\parallel
is the parallel operator.
With
(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})
, we restrict a new session
s
for which the CSM
\mathcal{A}
specifies the intended session behaviour.

\mathcal{A}
is ignored by our reduction semantics and solely used for type checking.
We have internal (
\IntCh
) and external (
\ExtCh
) choice and assume that
\lvert I\rvert>0
.
For runtime configurations, the use of processes
P_{i}
for continuations ensures that we only specify queue contents for active session restrictions.
A session restriction is
active
if it is not
guarded
by internal or external actions.

\mathsf{Q}[\vec{c}]
specifies the use of a process definition with identifier
\mathsf{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}
and parameters
\vec{c}
for which

\mathcal{D}
provides the definitions.
We only consider guarded process definitions (and
c
and
c_{i}
can be in
\vec{x}
).
This allows us to properly distinguish between processes and runtime configurations later:
in the reduction rules, we will only add queues for active session restrictions.
We assume that
\mathcal{D}
has one single definition for every process identifier in
\mathcal{Q}
.
Thus, we define
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Q},\vec{c})
as unfolding of the process definition when its variables
\vec{x}
are substituted by
\vec{c}
.
For runtime configurations,

s\blacktriangleright\sigma

denotes that the queues of session
s
are currently
\sigma
.
The function

\sigma\colon\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathit{Msg}^{*}

where

\mathit{Msg}\ni m\Coloneqq l\langle v\rangle

specifies the queue content where
l
is from a finite set of labels.
If all queues are empty, we exploit notation and use
\varepsilon
, i.e.
\varepsilon({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\coloneq\varepsilon
.
We will use the term
\mathbf{err}
to specify if something went wrong.

The correctness of our typing relies on the CSMs used for annotations
to be well-behaved, which we encode in the notion of well-annotated processes.

Definition 23
(Well-annotated)

We say a process/runtime configuration is
well-annotated
if
every CSM appearing in it is:

1.

deadlock-free, and

2.

satisfies feasible eventual reception.

Note that any CSM obtained through projection automatically satisfies
the conditions of well-annotation.

In our process calculus, recursion can be achieved using process definitions
\mathsf{Q}[\vec{x}]
.
Not that, for global and local types, recursion is usually defined using
\mu t
, which binds a recursion variable
t
that can be used subsequently
(cf.
Definitions

33
and
41
).

\displaystyle P\coloneq

\displaystyle\;(\upnu s_{1}:\mathcal{A})(\upnu s_{2}:\mathcal{A})\;P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}}\parallel P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}\parallel P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ where}

\displaystyle P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]!\textit{query}\langle\text{``Alice in Wonderland''}\rangle\,.\,s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]?\textit{price}(p)\,.\,

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{if}p>10

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{then}s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]!\textit{no}\,.\,\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{else}s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]!\textit{buy}\,.\,s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]!\textit{card}\langle\text{``1234..., 08/2024, 532''}\rangle\,.\,

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 65.87474pt\ExtCh\begin{cases}s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]?\textit{confirm}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\\
s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]?\textit{cancel}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

\displaystyle P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}]?\textit{query}(b)\,.\,s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}]!\textit{price}\langle\operatorname{prices}[b]\rangle\,.\,

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}\coloneq

An example process following the protocol of
Example

5
.

Example 6

In
Fig.

14
, we give a process
P
that uses
projections of the global types from

Example

5
:

\mathcal{A}
is a projection of
G_{1}
and

\mathcal{B}
is a projection of
G_{2}
.
We assume base types
Bool
,
str
and
int
as well as a construct for if-then-else for illustrative purposes.
In
P
,
\operatorname{prices}[b]
denotes a lookup for the price and
\operatorname{is-valid}\colon\texttt{str}\to\texttt{Bool}
is a function that checks if credit card details are valid.
Note the use of variable
y
in
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for the delegation.
In fact, it does not know the endpoint, or local type, it receives but needs to trust that it can perform the respective actions on it.
A type system can ensure this.

Definition 24

We define a function
\lceil\hbox{-}\rceil
to convert a process into a runtime configuration by adding channel types for active sessions:

\displaystyle\lceil P_{1}\parallel P_{2}\rceil

\displaystyle\coloneq\lceil P_{1}\rceil\parallel\lceil P_{2}\rceil

\displaystyle\lceil(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P\rceil

\displaystyle\coloneq(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,(\lceil P\rceil\parallel s\blacktriangleright\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\lceil P\rceil

\displaystyle\coloneq P\text{ otherwise}

We define structural (pre)congruence.
Intuitively, this shows which kind of transformations do not change the meaning of a process or runtime configuration.
For instance, parallel composition of
P
with
\mathbf{0}
is basically the same as
P
itself.

Definition 25

For processes, the rules for structural congruence
\equiv
are the following:

•

P_{1}\parallel P_{2}\equiv P_{2}\parallel P_{1}

•

(P_{1}\parallel P_{2})\parallel P_{3}\equiv P_{1}\parallel(P_{2}\parallel P_{3})

•

P\parallel\mathbf{0}\equiv P

•

(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,(\upnu s^{\prime}:\mathcal{B})\,P\equiv(\upnu s^{\prime}:\mathcal{B})\,(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P

•

(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,(P_{1}\parallel P_{2})\equiv P_{1}\parallel(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P_{2}
,
if
s
is not free in
P_{1}

We define structural precongruence
\sqsubseteq
for processes as the smallest precongruence relation that
includes
\equiv
and

(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,\mathbf{0}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{0}
.
For runtime configurations, the rules for structural congruence
\equiv
are the ones above.
We define structural precongruence
\sqsubseteq
for runtime configurations as the smallest precongruence relation that
includes
\equiv

and

(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,s\blacktriangleright\varepsilon\sqsubseteq\mathbf{0}
.

We only define one direction for the rules

(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,\mathbf{0}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{0}

and

(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,s\blacktriangleright\varepsilon\sqsubseteq\mathbf{0}
.
This is solely required to prove that structural congruence preserves typability for both processes and runtime configurations (cf.
Lemmas

13
and
14
).
Intuitively, the other direction would require to impose conditions on the CSM
\mathcal{A}
.
This treatment is not restrictive in terms of reductions: applying these rules from right to left will not change the possibility for reductions.

Last, we define the reduction rules for our process calculus.

Definition 26

For our reduction rules, we first define a reduction context:

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbb{C}\parallel R|R\parallel\mathbb{C}|(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,\mathbb{C}|[\,]

In
Fig.

15
we define the reduction rules.
The rule
RR-
\mathsf{Q}
unfolds a process definition while
RR-ctx
allows us to descend for reductions using contexts.
Both rules
RR-out
and
RR-in
specify how a message is output to a queue or received as input from a queue.

RR-
\sqsubseteq
allows us to consider structurally precongruent runtime configurations for reductions.

RR-err1
yields an error if the next action is to receive but all possible incoming messages do not match any specified label.
Last,
RR-err2
yields an error if a session is over but there are non-empty queues for this session.

and }l_{i}\neq l}{\ExtCh_{i\in }\par\frac{\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\neq\varepsilon\text{ for some }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}{(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\;s\blacktriangleright\sigma\longrightarrow\mathbf{err}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right={RR-err2} }

Figure 15
:
Reduction rules for runtime configurations.

Remark 2

To prove the absence of runtime error
RR-err1
, we require that feasible eventual reception holds.
If the CSM does not have this property, there might be messages that could be left behind.

Example 7

We give a reduction for the process specified in

Example

6
.
First, we apply the function
\lceil\hbox{-}\rceil
to turn the process into a runtime configuration, yielding

R\coloneq(\upnu s_{1}:\mathcal{A})(\upnu s_{2}:\mathcal{B})\,P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}}\parallel P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}\parallel P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\parallel s_{1}\blacktriangleright\varepsilon\parallel s_{2}\blacktriangleright\varepsilon

There is only one possible reduction step: the message
\textit{query}\langle\text{``Alice in Wonderland''}\rangle
is sent by
s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}]
to
s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]
.
Then, we obtain the following runtime configuration:

\displaystyle R^{\prime}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;(\upnu s_{1}:\mathcal{A})(\upnu s_{2}:\mathcal{B})\;P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}}\parallel P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}\parallel P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

\displaystyle\;\hskip 77.4997pt\parallel s_{1}\blacktriangleright\varepsilon[({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}})\mapsto\textit{query}\langle\text{``Alice in Wonderland''}\rangle]\parallel s_{2}\blacktriangleright\varepsilon

where

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]?\textit{price}(p)\,.\,

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{if}p>10

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{then}s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]!\textit{no}\,.\,\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{else}s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]!\textit{buy}\,.\,s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]!\textit{card}\langle\text{``1234..., 08/2024, 111''}\rangle\,.\,\ExtCh\begin{cases}s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]?\textit{confirm}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\\
s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{a}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}]?\textit{cancel}\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

Despite dealing with runtime configurations, we can specify processes because the queues are specified at top level.

0.C.3
Type System for Processes and Runtime Configurations

Typing for base types is well-understood.
Thus, we focus on the more difficult case of delegation, following work by

Scalas and Yoshida [69]
.
Integration of base types is mostly orthogonal and would distract from the main concerns here so we briefly remark differences in the treatment of base types after presenting our type system.

For processes, we have two typing contexts:

\Theta
and
\Lambda
.
We consider states as syntactic markers for local specifications so we use
L
as type for such payloads. So far, we only considered a fixed set of participants
\mathcal{P}
.
In a system with multiple CSMs, we write
\mathcal{P}_{\!\mathcal{A}}
to denote the subset of participants of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathsf{Chan}_{\mathcal{A}}
for the respective channels.
We might also use the session
s
instead of the respective CSM.

Prior to giving definitions for our type system, let us remark that we make use of the
Barendregt Variable Convention

[
9
]
, which assumes that the names of bound variables is always distinct from the ones of free variables.
This allows us not to explicitly rename variables, simplifying the formalisation both for writing and reading.

Definition 27

The process definition typing context
\Theta
is a function from process identifiers to types for its parameters:

\Theta\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\vec{L}
.
A
syntactic typing context
is defined by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\Lambda,s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:L|\Lambda,x:L|\emptyset

A syntactic typing context is a
typing context
if every element has at most one type.
Here, we do only consider typing contexts.
We consider typing contexts to be equivalent up to reordering and, thus, we may also treat them as mappings.
We use notation
\{\Lambda_{i}\}_{i\in I}
to denote that we split
\Lambda
into
\lvert I\rvert
typing contexts.

Equipped with these typing contexts, we can give the typing rules for processes.
The first two rules solely deal with the process definition typing context, which provides the types for process definitions.
The other rules deal with the different constructs of our process calculus and how to type them.
Most importantly, our type system ensures that all information in the typing context is used exactly once.

\frac{}{\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\varepsilon:\Theta}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-def-$\varepsilon$} }\par\frac{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\vec{x}:\vec{L}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta(\mathsf{Q})=\vec{L}}{\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;(\mathsf{Q}[\vec{x}]=P);\mathcal{D}:\Theta}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-def} }\par\frac{\Theta(\mathsf{Q})=\vec{L}}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\vec{c}:\vec{L}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\mathsf{Q}[\vec{c}]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\mathsf{Q}$} }\par\frac{}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\mathbf{0}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\mathbf{0}$} }\par\frac{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{end}(q)}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}c:q,\Lambda\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-end} }\par\frac{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda_{1}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda_{2}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P_{1}\parallel P_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\parallel$} }\par\frac{\delta(q)\supseteq\{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!l_{i}(L_{i}),q_{i})\mid i\in I\}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\forall i\in I.\,}\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q_{i},\{c_{j}:L_{j}\}_{j\in I\setminus\{i\}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P_{i}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q,\{c_{i}:L_{i}\}_{i\in I}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\IntCh$} }\par\frac{\delta(q)=\{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?l_{i}(L_{i}),q_{i})\mid i\in I\}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\forall i\in I.\,}\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q_{i},y_{i}:L_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P_{i}\hskip 9.24994pt}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\ExtCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]?l_{i}(y_{i})\,.\,P_{i}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\ExtCh$} }\par\frac{\Lambda_{s}=\{s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:\operatorname{init}(\mathcal{A}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}})\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\mathcal{A}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,\Lambda_{s}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={PT-$\upnu$} }

Figure 16:

The typing rules for processes.

\operatorname{init}(\hbox{-})
denotes the initial state of a CSM.

Definition 28

We define
\operatorname{end}(q)
to hold when
q
is final and does not have outgoing receive transitions.
The typing rules for processes are shown in
Fig.

16
.
We assume that all CSMs in typing derivations are deadlock-free and satisfy feasible eventual reception.

The rules
PT-def-
\varepsilon
and
PT-def
ensure that the process definition typing context provides the right types for parameters.
This is then used to type process definitions in a process, using
PT-
\mathsf{Q}
.

PT-
\mathbf{0}
types
\mathbf{0}
with an empty second typing context while
PT-end
can be used to remove type bindings
c:q
where
q
is a final state without outgoing receive transitions.
The rules
PT-
\IntCh
and
PT-
\ExtCh
can be used to type internal and external choice.
The rule
PT-
\parallel
allows us to split the typing contexts and type the respective processes independently.
Last,
PT-
\upnu
adds type bindings for a session
s
and requires that the remaining process is typed using this.

Our type system is
linear
, i.e. it requires that every type binding is used once and they can only be dropped if they correspond to final states without outgoing receive transitions.
This ensures that all the actions specified by the CSM are actually taken and the participants of a session cannot stop earlier.

It might seem that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
PT-
\IntCh
and
PT-
\ExtCh
is unbound but, by assumption,
q
is distinct across all considered FSMs so it is clear from context.
Let us explain
PT-
\IntCh
in more detail.
To type

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q,\{c_{i}:L_{i}\}_{i\in I}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;\IntCh_{i\in I}c[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}]!l_{i}\langle c_{i}\rangle\,.\,P_{i}
,
we require all send actions to be possible from
q
, i.e.

\delta(q)\supseteq\{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!l_{i}(L_{i}),q_{i})\mid i\in I\}
.
We do not require all of them to be possible though, in contrast to the receive actions in
PT-
\ExtCh
.
In addition, for every
i\in I
, we require the following:

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda,c:q_{i},\{c_{j}:L_{j}\}_{j\in I\setminus\{i\}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash}\;P_{i}
,
which gives
c
the new binding
q_{i}
and removes the type binding for the payload
c_{i}:L_{i}
.
Intuitively, it is transferred when sending a message.
Thus, in its counterpart
PT-
\ExtCh
, the payload’s type will be used to type the continuation after receiving it, i.e.
y_{i}:L_{i}
.

The assumption that we only consider typing contexts, and not syntactic typing contexts, ensures that
\Lambda
in the conclusion of
PT-
\upnu
does not contain any
s
for instance.
The same will hold for the typing rules for runtime configurations.

Remark 3
(
\operatorname{end}(\hbox{-})
and final non-sink states)

We use
\operatorname{end}(q)
to check if
q
is a final state without outgoing receive transitions.
Following standard MST frameworks, we would simply require
q
to be final.
Thus, our type system is slightly more general.
However, when using our type system for projections from our approach, this will not be exploited:
we base our projection on results by

[
57
]
and their (complete) conditions do not allow final state with outgoing send transitions.

.

Example 8

Let us illustrate delegation with an example.
We have the following global type:

G_{1}\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptl(\texttt{end})\,.\,\mathbf{0}

for which we could model the payload of
l(\texttt{end})
with an arbitrary state
end
such that
\operatorname{end}(\textit{end}\,)
.
For readability, we omit its treatment in the typing derivations.
We obtain a projection
\mathcal{A}
of
G_{1}
,
giving
Fig.

17(a)
and
Fig.

17(b)
.
Using the states of these, we define delegation in the second global type:

G_{2}\coloneq+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993ptl_{1}(q_{0})\,.\,\mathbf{0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{\to}\kern 0.36993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\kern 0.36993pt{:}\kern 0.36993ptl_{2}(\texttt{end})\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

A projection
\mathcal{B}
for
G_{2}
is given in

Fig.

17(c)
and

Fig.

17(d)
.
Let us define a process that uses both global types:

\displaystyle P\coloneq

\displaystyle\;(\upnu s_{1}:\mathcal{A})(\upnu s_{2}:\mathcal{B})\;P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\parallel P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}\parallel P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}\text{ where}

\displaystyle P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;\IntCh\begin{cases}s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]!l_{1}\langle s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]\rangle\,.\,\mathbf{0}\\
s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]!l_{2}\langle\textit{end}\,\rangle\,.\,s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]!l\langle\textit{end}\,\rangle\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

\displaystyle P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;s_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]?l(x)\,.\,\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle P_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}\coloneq

\displaystyle\;\ExtCh\begin{cases}s_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]?l_{1}(x)\,.\,x[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]!l\langle\textit{end}\,\rangle\,.\,\mathbf{0}\\
s_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]?l_{2}(x)\,.\,\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

In this example, the process definition typing context is always empty so we omit it.
To fit it within the page limits, we give the typing derivation in pieces.
We use numbers
(0)-(5)
to refer to the typing derivation for the respective branch.
It should be read from bottom to top, starting from
(0)
.
We start with the initial part until typing we arrive at typing the parallel composition.

8.19447pt\textsc{right={PT-$\ExtCh$} }

During runtime, we have queues for each session.
For these, we define queue types and use them in queue typing contexts.

Definition 29

Queue types are defined by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\Coloneqq l(L)\cdot\gamma|\varepsilon

A
syntactic queue typing context
is defined by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\Omega,s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]::\gamma|\emptyset

A syntactic queue typing context is a
queue typing context
if every element has at most one type.
Here, we do only consider queue typing contexts.
We consider queue typing contexts to be equivalent up to reordering and, thus, we may also treat them as mappings.

While the (second) typing context specifies states for each participant, the queue typing context specifies the content of the queues.
This is all we need to define reductions following the respective communicating state machine.

Definition 30

We define the reductions for typing contexts as follows:

\frac{q\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!l(L)}q^{\prime}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q,\Lambda\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]::\gamma,\Omega\longrightarrow s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q^{\prime},\Lambda\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]::\gamma\cdot l(L),\Omega}\quad\textsc{right={TR-$\IntCh$} }\par\frac{q\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?l(L)}q^{\prime}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q,\Lambda\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]::l(L)\cdot\gamma,\Omega\longrightarrow s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q^{\prime},\Lambda\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]::\gamma,\Omega}\quad\textsc{right={TR-$\ExtCh$} }

These rules mimic exactly the semantics of communicating state machines.

We show that reductions for typing contexts are preserved when adding type bindings to the typing contexts.

Lemma 12

Let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}
, and
\Lambda_{2}
be typing contexts and

\Omega_{1},\Omega^{\prime}_{1}
, and
\Omega_{2}
be queue typing contexts.
If

\Lambda_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega_{1}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega^{\prime}_{1}
,
then

\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}\longrightarrow\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega_{1}^{\prime},\Omega_{2}
.

Proof

We do inversion on

\Lambda_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega_{1}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Omega^{\prime}_{1}
, yielding two cases.
First, we have

\small\frac{q\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!l(L)}q^{\prime}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q,\hat{\Lambda}_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]::\gamma,\hat{\Omega}_{1}\longrightarrow s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q^{\prime},\hat{\Lambda}_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]::\gamma\cdot l(L),\hat{\Omega}_{1}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={TR-$\IntCh$} }

With this, it is obvious that the following holds:

\small\frac{q\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!l(L)}q^{\prime}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q,\hat{\Lambda}_{1},\Lambda_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]::\gamma,\hat{\Omega}_{1},\Omega_{2}\longrightarrow s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}]:q^{\prime},\hat{\Lambda}_{1},\Lambda_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}s[{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}]::\gamma\cdot l(L),\hat{\Omega}_{1},\Omega_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={TR-$\IntCh$} }

which is precisely what we have to show.
The case for
TR-
\ExtCh
is analogous and, therefore, omitted.

\square

After this small intermezzo on reductions for typing contexts, we now define the typing rules for runtime configurations.

l\langle v\rangle\cdot\vec{m}]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={RT-Queue} }

Figure 18:
Typing rules for runtime configurations;

\operatorname{reach}(\hbox{-})
denotes the set of reachable configurations of the given CSM.

Definition 31

The typing rules for runtime configurations are defined in
Fig.

18
.
We require all CSMs in typing derivations to be deadlock-free and to satisfy feasible eventual reception.

Most rules are analogous to the rules for processes.
For
RT-
\upnu
, though, we do not require the CSM configuration to be initial but solely reachable, yielding typability of runtime configurations during execution.
The rules for queues are standard:

RT-Queue
types queues from the first to the last message in the queue while

RT-EmptyQueue
types empty queues.

\mathbf{0}

We give the typing derivation for
