From subject reduction and session fidelity, deadlock freedom would easily follow.

Lemma 23
(Deadlock freedom with sink-final FSMs)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a deadlock-free CSM that satisfies feasible eventual reception and,
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\mathcal{A}}
,

\mathcal{A}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is sink-final.
Let
P
be a process. Assume that

•

\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash_{\tiny{SF}}}\;\mathcal{D}:\Theta
,

•

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash_{\tiny{SF}}}\;(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P
,

•

\mathcal{A}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is sink-final for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\mathcal{A}}
, and

•

\lceil(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P\rceil\longrightarrow^{*}R
.

Then, it holds that
R\sqsubseteq\mathbf{0}
or there is
R^{\prime}
such that
R\longrightarrow R^{\prime}
.

Proof Sketch.

First, we claim that for all
k\geq 0
with

\lceil(\upnu s:\mathcal{A})\,P\rceil\longrightarrow^{k}R
,
it holds that

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\Lambda\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\shortmid}\emptyset\overset{(\vec{q},\xi)}{\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\vdash_{\tiny{SF}}}\;}R

for some

\vec{q}
and
\xi
.
This can be shown using subject reduction
Theorem

0.C.1
.
We do a case analysis if there is

(\vec{q}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})

with

(\vec{q},\xi)\longrightarrow(\vec{q}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
.

If so, we know from

Theorem

0.C.2

that there is
R^{\prime}
with
R\longrightarrow R^{\prime}
, which concludes this case.

Suppose that there is no

(\vec{q}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})

with

(\vec{q},\xi)\longrightarrow(\vec{q}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
.
By the assumption that
\mathcal{A}
is deadlock-free and, thus, all
\vec{q}
are final states (and
\xi
only has empty channels).

Appendix 0.D
Additional Material for
Section

6

We structure our formalisation in subsections, aligning with the key consequences

(a)

to

(d)
,
explained in the main text.
Combining these and previous results, we will close this section by proving undecidability of the projectability problem and the strong projectability problem in the presence of mixed choice.

0.D.1
Additional Material for Consequence
(a)

We present syntax and semantics of global types following
[
70
]
.

Definition 33
(Syntax of global types)

Global types
for MSTs are defined by the grammar:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\mathbf{0}|\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{i}\,.\,G_{i}|μt\,.\,G|t

The term
\mathbf{0}
explicitly represents termination while
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{i}
indicates an interaction where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}
sends message
m_{i}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
.
We assume
\lvert I\rvert>0
and, if
\lvert I\rvert=1
, we omit the sum operator.
The operators
\mu t
and
t
can be used to encode loops.
We require them to be guarded, i.e., there must be at least one interaction between the binding
\mu t
and the use of the recursion variable
t
.
Without loss of generality, all occurrences of recursion variables
t
are bound and distinct.
A global type satisfies
mixed choice
if for each
syntactic subterm

\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{i}\,.\,G_{i}
,
its branches are unique, i.e.
∀i,j∈I.\,i≠j⇒{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{j}\lor{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}\lor m_{i}≠m_{j}
;
otherwise it is
non-deterministic
.
Directed choice requires the sender and receiver to be the same but messages to be distinct for branches:

\forall i,j\in I.\,\,i\neq j⇒{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{j}\land{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}\land m_{i}\neq m_{j}
.
In contrast, sender-driven choice requires each receiver-message pair to be distinct:

\forall i,j∈I.\,\,i≠j⇒{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{j}\land({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}\lor m_{i}≠m_{j})
.
We may say that a global type is directed or sender-driven.

Figure

3
represents a global type from MSTs.

Definition 34
(Semantics of global types)

Let
G
be a global type.
We index every syntactic subterm of
G
with a unique index to distinguish common syntactic subterms, denoted with
[G,k]
for syntactic subterm
G
and index
k
.
Without loss of generality, the index for
G
is
1
:

[G,1]
.
We define

\mathsf{GAut}(G)=(Q_{\mathsf{GAut}(G)},\Sigma,δ_{\mathsf{GAut}(G)},q_{0,\mathsf{GAut}(G)},F_{\mathsf{GAut}(G)})
where

•

Q_{\mathsf{GAut}(G)}
is the set of all indexed syntactic subterms
[G,k]
of
G
,

•

δ_{\mathsf{GAut}(G)}
is the smallest set containing

([\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{i}.[G_{i},k_{i}],k],{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm_{i},[G_{i},k_{i}])
for each
i∈I
,

([μt.[G^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{2}],k^{\prime}_{1}],ε,[G^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{2}])
and
([t,k^{\prime}_{3}],ε,[μt.[G^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{2}],k^{\prime}_{1}])
,

•

q_{0,\mathsf{GAut}(G)}=[G,1]
, and

F_{\mathsf{GAut}(G)}=\{[\mathbf{0},k]\mid k\text{ is an index for subterm }\mathbf{0}\}
.

We obtain the semantics using
\sim
:

\mathcal{L}(G)\coloneq\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{GAut}(G)))
.

For every global type
G
,
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
, when viewed as a PSM, satisfies a number of properties, which were defined in

[
70
, Def. 3.5]
.

Definition 35
(Ancestor-recursive, non-merging, intermediate recursion, etc.)

Let
A=(Q,\Delta,\delta,q_{0},F)
be a finite state machine.
For convenience, we write
q\rightarrow q^{\prime}
if
q\xrightarrow{x}q^{\prime}
for some
x\in\Delta
.
We say that
A
is
ancestor-recursive
if there is a function
\operatorname{lvl}\colon Q\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for every transition
q\xrightarrow{x}q^{\prime}\in\delta
, one of the two holds:

(i)

\operatorname{lvl}(q)>\operatorname{lvl}(q^{\prime})
, or

(ii)

x=\varepsilon
and there is a run from the initial state
q_{0}
(without going through
q
) to
q^{\prime}
which can be completed to reach
q
:

q_{0}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow q_{n}
is a run with
q_{n}=q^{\prime}
and
q\neq q_{i}
for every
0\leq i\leq n
, and
the run can be extended to

q_{0}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow q_{n}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow q_{n+m}
with
q_{n+m}=q
.
Then, the state
q^{\prime}
is called
ancestor
of
q
.

We call the first (i) kind of transition
forward transition
while the second (ii) kind is a
backward transition
.
The state machine
A
is said to be free from
intermediate recursion
if every state
q
with more than one outgoing transition, i.e.,

\lvert\{q^{\prime}\mid q\rightarrow q^{\prime}\in\delta\}\rvert>1
,
has only forward transitions.
We say that
A
is
non-merging
if every state only has one incoming edge with greater level, i.e., for every state
q^{\prime}
,
\{q\mid q\rightarrow q^{\prime}\in\delta\land\operatorname{lvl}(q)>\operatorname{lvl}(q^{\prime})\}\leq 1
.

Stutz [70, Prop. 3.6]
show that state machines for sender-driven global types satisfy the above properties.
It is straightforward that this also holds for mixed-choice and non-deterministic global types.

Proposition 4

Let
G
be a global type.
Then,
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
is a sink-final, ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense
Σ1-PSM
without intermediate recursion.
If
G
is non-deterministic, mixed-choice, sender-driven or directed, so is
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
.

0.D.2
Additional Material for Consequence

(b)

Every global type’s state machine is a sink-final tree-like
Σ1-PSM
, when viewed as a PSM.
This raises an obvious question.
For which kind of PSMs can we have global types that have the same (core) language?
And can we preserve the various restrictions on choice, e.g. sender-driven choice, for those?
Such preservation is particularly interesting in the light of our undecidability result for projectability of mixed-choice Σ1-PSMs.
It is immediate that we can only achieve this for Σ1-PSMs because we consider the core language and not the semantics.

Anti-patterns.

Visually, we want to transform a
Σ1-PSM
with an arbitrary structure into a tree-like structure where recursion only happens at leaves and to ancestors.
There are several anti-patterns one needs to consider: e.g. mutual recursion, intermediate recursion, and merging.
As is standard for a tree-like shape, we assume states have different levels:
the initial state has the highest level and the level usually decreases when taking a transition.
If not, it is considered recursion.
First, recursion is supposed to lead to an ancestor, i.e. a state from which the state itself can be reached without increasing the level again; if this is not the case, we call this
mutual recursion
.
Second, recursion should happen at a leaf, i.e. there is no outgoing transition to a state with smaller level;
if there is such a transition, there is
intermediate recursion
.
Third, every state ought to have at most one incoming transition;
if not, we call this
merging
.

Naive approach breaks choice restrictions.

It is rather easy to remove these anti-patterns by duplicating various parts of the
Σ1-PSM
.
This will easily introduce non-determinism though, defeating the goal of preserving restrictions on choice.
With this in mind, the problem becomes significantly more challenging.

Overview of our workflow.

We develop a workflow that transforms Σ1-PSMs to tree-like Σ1-PSMs with recursion at leaves and to ancestors, which are easily turned into global types.
Let us first give a very high-level overview and report on technical challenges.
The key insight to establish the desirable properties is the use of regular expressions as intermediate representations.
It is well-known that Arden’s Lemma
[
6
]
can be used to transform an FSM into a regular expression,
but it produces one regular expression for every final state.
We flip Arden’s lemma, prove it correct, and use it to produce a regular expression for the (only) initial state.
This is only sound as we solely consider sink-final Σ1-PSMs.
(Our results can only preserve the restrictions of choice for sink-final Σ1-PSMs, which is reasonable because global types as PSMs are always sink-final.)
To prove the preservation of choice restrictions, we also define these for regular expressions over
\Gamma
, inspired by deterministic regular expressions by
Brüggemann-Klein and
Wood [16]
.
Let us now explain how regular expressions help to establish the desirable properties.
Intuitively, one can traverse a regular expression bottom-up and generate an FSM for the same language.
The expression for alternative
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
becomes a branch to the two respective FSMs for
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
and
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
.
For concatenation
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
, we simply connect both FSMs, and for Kleene star
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}^{*}
we make the initial state final and add transitions from the final to the initial state.
While this gives an idea of our approach, such treatment still introduces (undesirable) non-determinism to connect different FSMs.
To avoid this, we employ Brzozowski derivatives
[
17
]
, but adapt them to PSMs.
They allow us to pull the first event out so we can use labelled transitions to connect the FSMs.
Of course, we also prove that these PSM derivatives preserve the restrictions on choice.

Our workflow comprises the following steps:

(0)

make the PSM sink-final for the price of introducing non-determinism

(1)

compute a regular expression for the initial state of the sink-final PSM

(2)

convert regular expression to a PSM that is ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense, and intermediate-recursion-free

(3)

if the original PSM is a
Σ1-PSM
,
transform the result from the previous step to a global type

Without loss of generality, we assume that every sink state is final:
any state, for which this is not the case, can simply be removed while preserving the core language and semantics of a PSM.

For the last step, we only consider Σ1-PSMs because global types always jointly specify send and receive events.

Remark 5

Our constructions do also apply to FSMs over other alphabets.
For these, the reasoning about preserving sender-driven choice often translates to preserving determinism.
Thus, it shows that the above structural conditions do not change expressivity for sink-final deterministic FSMs.
For FSMs for participants of protocols, this establishes a connection to local types, as shown later.

Step
(0)
: Sink State iff Final State.

This can be considered to be a preprocessing step for PSMs that are not sink-final, making the workflow more general.
This transformation step simply introduces a new final sink state to which transitions can lead non-deterministically.

We give a construction with a single fresh final state, which can be (non-deterministically) reached instead of any previous final state.

{procedure}

[PSM: Sink State iff Final State]

Let
M=(Q,\Gamma,\delta,q_{0},F)
be a PSM with
\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{S}(M)
.
We define a function that turns
M
into a sink-final PSM:

\operatorname{psm2sink-final-psm}(M)\coloneq(Q\uplus\{q_{f}\},\Gamma,\delta^{\prime},q_{0},\{q_{f}\})

where

(q_{1},x,q_{2})\in\delta^{\prime}
if
(q_{1},x,q_{2})\in\delta
as well as

(q_{1},x,q_{f})\in\delta^{\prime}
if
(q_{1},x,q_{2})\in\delta
and
q_{2}\in F
.

The condition that
\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{S}(M)
ensures that there is a predecessor state for every final state to which we can add the transition.

Proposition 5

Let
Σ1-PSM
be a
M
such that
\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{S}(M)
.
Then, the PSM
\operatorname{psm2sink-final-psm}(M)
is sink-final.

It is obvious that this construction introduces and, thus, does not preserve sender-driven choice.

Step
(1)
: From Sink-final PSMs to Regular Expressions.

This transformation step translates a sink-final PSM to a regular expression over
\Gamma
that specifies the same core language.
It is well-known that this can be done using Arden’s Lemma
[
6
]
.
We cannot apply the standard technique though, as it would produce as many regular expressions as final states.
Such treatment makes it very hard to argue about the preservation of sender-driven choice.
Instead, we exploit the fact that the PSM is sink-final and produce a single regular expression for the initial state.
This also enables the treatment of infinite words, which solely require an infinite run that necessarily does not end in a final state.

We define regular expressions and include infinite words in their semantics.

Definition 36
(Regular Expressions)

Let
\Delta
be an alphabet.
Regular expressions (REs) over
\Delta
are inductively defined by the following grammar where
a\in\Delta
:

\displaystyle{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\varepsilon|a|{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}|{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}|{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}^{*}

The concatenation operator
\cdot
has precedence over
+
.
We define

\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}(a)=\{a\}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})=\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1})\cup\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})
,

\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})=\{w_{1}\cdot w_{2}\mid w_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}),w_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})\}
,
and

\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}^{*})=\{w_{1}\ldots w_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N},\forall i\leq n.\,w_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\}
.
The infinite language
\mathcal{L}_{\inf}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is defined as

\{w\in\Delta^{\omega}\mid\forall w^{\prime}\in\operatorname{pref}(w).\,w^{\prime}\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}))\}
.
The language
\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is the union of
\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\inf}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
.
The function
\operatorname{sym}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is the set of all letters in
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
, i.e. the smallest subset
\Delta^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta
such that
\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\subseteq(\Delta^{\prime})^{\infty}
.
We denote the set of all regular expressions over
\Delta
with
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
.

Instead of constructing the regular expressions for final states, as is standard with Arden’s Lemma, we construct one for the initial state.
This is sound because a state is a sink if and only if it is final.
It also lets us handle infinite words.
For a state machine, an infinite word is part of its semantics if there is an infinite run.
Here, we mimic this: an infinite word is in the semantics of a regular expression if every prefix of the word is a prefix of a word in the finite semantics.

Lemma 24
(Arden’s Lemma – swapped)

Let
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
and
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
be two regular expressions over an alphabet
\Delta
.
If
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
does not contain the empty string,
i.e.
\varepsilon\not\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1})
, then

{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3}={r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}+({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3})
has a unique solution that is
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3}={r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}^{*}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
.

Proof

The proof is analogous to the original one:

\displaystyle{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3}

\displaystyle={r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3}

\displaystyle={r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3})

\displaystyle={r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3}

\displaystyle=\ldots

\displaystyle={r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}^{2}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}^{3}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt3}+\ldots

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}^{2}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}^{3}+\ldots)\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}

\displaystyle={r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}^{*}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}

\square

For sender-driven PSMs, we want to show that sender-driven choice is preserved.
Therefore, we need a notion of sender-driven choice for regular expressions.
We define this following work on deterministic regular expressions
[
16
]
.

Definition 37
(Marking and unmarking regular expressions)

Let
\Delta
be an alphabet and
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}\in\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}
be a regular expression.
We define a function
\texttt{mark}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
that simply subscripts every letter in
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
with a distinct index and the inverse function
\texttt{unmark}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
, which is also defined for words over
\Delta
.

Definition 38
(Mixed-choice, sender-driven and directed regular expressions)

Let
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}\in\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
.
We say that
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
is a
sender-driven
regular expression if the following holds:
for every
u\in\Gamma^{*}
and
x,y\in\Gamma
,
if
ux\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\texttt{mark}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})))
,
uy\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\texttt{mark}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})))
and
x\neq y
, then

\texttt{unmark}(x)\neq\texttt{unmark}(y)

as well as

\texttt{unmark}(x)\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}\}

and

\texttt{unmark}(y)\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}\}

for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.
For
directed choice
, we also require
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
to be the same for both
x
and
y
and, for
mixed choice
, we solely require
\texttt{unmark}(x)\neq\texttt{unmark}(y)
.

Compared to deterministic regular expressions, our definition requires the special alphabet
\Gamma
(and adds a condition for sender-driven and directed choice).

Proposition 6

Every mixed-choice, sender-driven or directed RE is a deterministic RE.

Definition 39

Let
\Delta
be an alphabet and
L\subseteq\Delta^{\infty}
.
We define a function that collects all first letters of
L
:

\texttt{first}(L)\coloneq\operatorname{pref}(L)\cap\Delta
.
The function
\texttt{follow}(L,a)
collects all letters that can occur after
a
in
L
:

\texttt{follow}(L,a)\coloneq\{b\mid wab\in\operatorname{pref}(L)\}

and

\texttt{follow}(L,\varepsilon)\coloneq\texttt{first}(L)
.

The following lemma follows from a straightforward adaption of Lemma 2.2 by

Brüggemann-Klein and
Wood [16]
.

Lemma 25

An RE
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}\in\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma}
is a sender-driven RE if and only if,
for every
z\in\operatorname{sym}(\texttt{mark}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}))\uplus\{\varepsilon\}
and
every
x,y\in\texttt{follow}(\texttt{mark}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}),z)
,
if
x\neq y
,
then
\texttt{unmark}(x)\neq\texttt{unmark}(y)
,
as well as

\texttt{unmark}(x)\in\mathcal{L}(\Gamma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})

and

\texttt{unmark}(y)\in\mathcal{L}(\Gamma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})

for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

Intuitively, one can check if an RE over
\Gamma
is an sender-driven RE as follows.
For every subexpression of the form
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
and
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}^{*}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
,
the REs
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
and
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
should not share any first letters and the union of their first letters belongs to the same participant.
It suffices to consider these operators as these are the only ones where lookahead to take a decision about the path in the RE is needed.

{procedure}

[PSM to RE]
Let
M=(Q,\Gamma,\delta,q_{0},F)
be a sink-final PSM.
We generate a system of equations.
For every
q_{1}\in Q
, we introduce
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt\negthinspace q_{1}}
as follows:

{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt\negthinspace q_{1}}=\sum\limits_{(q_{1},x,q_{2})\in\delta}x\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt\negthinspace q_{2}}

Given the initial state
q_{0}
, we can solve the system of equations for
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt\negthinspace q_{0}}
with
Lemma

24
, yielding a regular expression
\operatorname{psm2regex}(M)
.

The following lemma states the correctness of the previous procedure.

Lemma 26

For every sink-final PSM
M
,
it holds that
\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{psm2regex}(M))=\mathcal{L}(M)
.
If
M
is a sender-driven PSM, then
\operatorname{psm2regex}(M)
is a sender-driven RE.
If
\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{S}(M)
,
then
\varepsilon
does not occur in

\operatorname{psm2regex}(M)
.

Proof

With the assumption that
M
is sink-final, the first claim easily follows from
Lemma

24
.
For the second claim, let us investigate how the system of equations, from which
\operatorname{psm2regex}(M)
is obtained, is solved.
We observe that every equation is guarded, i.e. there is a letter from
\Sigma
before an occurrence of
{r}_{\kern-0.40974ptq}
for some state
q
.
Solving the system of equations for the initial state
{r}_{\kern-0.40974ptq_{0}}
can only involve substitution and the application of
Lemma

24
.
For both, sender-driven choice of
M
is preserved for the RE across all equations, yielding a sender-driven RE for
{r}_{\kern-0.40974ptq_{0}}
.
For the third claim, it suffices to observe that no
\varepsilon
is introduced in the system of equations.

\square

Step
(2)
: From Regular Expressions to Ancestor-recursive Non-merging Dense Intermediate-recursion-free PSMs.

After
the transformation, we want the PSM to be ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense and intermediate-recursion-free by construction.
We need to carefully design this transformation because the standard approach introduces non-determinism, for instance for union.
Resolving this non-determinism would easily break the desired structural properties, making the whole workflow pointless.
We apply the idea of derivatives in order not to introduce non-determinism.
To preserve sender-driven choice, we also ensure that sender-driven regular expressions are closed under Brzozowski Derivatives.
Given a regular expression
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
and a letter
a
, we can use them to construct a regular expression that specifies the language of words in the semantics of
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
which start with
a
and omits
a
.
We apply a similar idea to PSMs in order not to introduce non-determinism when constructing PSMs from regular expressions.

Definition 40
(
[
17
]
)

Let
\Delta
be an alphabet.
We define the Brzozowski derivative

\operatorname{brz-deriv}\colon\Delta\times\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}

as follows:

\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt})\coloneq\begin{cases}\varepsilon&\text{if }{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt}=a\\
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1})+\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt2})&\text{if }{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt}={r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1}+{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt2}\\
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1})\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt2}&\text{if }{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt}={r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt2}\land\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1})\\
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1})\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt2}+\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt2})&\text{if }{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt}={r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt2}\land\varepsilon\in\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1})\\
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1})\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1}^{*}&\text{if }{r}_{\kern-0.40251pt}={r}_{\kern-0.40251pt1}^{*}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Lemma 27
(Correctness of Brzozowski Derivatives
[
17
]
)

Let
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
be a regular expression over an alphabet
\Delta
and
a\in\Delta
be a letter.
If
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is defined, it holds that

\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}(\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}))=\{w\mid aw\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\}\kern 5.0pt.

If
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is not defined, it holds that
\{w\mid aw\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\}=\emptyset
.

We extend this result to infinite words.

Lemma 28
(Brzozowski Derivatives for infinite words)

Let
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
be a regular expression over an alphabet
\Delta
and
a\in\Delta
be a letter.
If
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is defined, it holds that

\mathcal{L}_{\inf}(\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}))=\{w\mid aw\in\mathcal{L}_{\inf}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\}\kern 5.0pt.

If
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is not defined, it holds that
\{w\mid aw\in\mathcal{L}_{\inf}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\}=\emptyset
.

Proof

For the first claim,
we consider infinite words.
By definition, an infinite word is in a language if all its prefixes are a prefix of some word in the finite language.
Thus, it suffices to show that

\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}(\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})))=\operatorname{pref}(\{w\mid aw\in\mathcal{L}_{\inf}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\}\cap\Delta^{*})
.
By definition, the prefixes of infinite words and finite words are the same for a regular expression.
Thus, it remains to show that

\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}(\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})))=\operatorname{pref}(\{w\mid aw\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\}\cap\Delta^{*})\kern 5.0pt.

This follows from

Lemma

27
.

The second claim simply follows from

Lemma

27
and the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\inf}(\hbox{-})
, which requires
\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}(\hbox{-})
to be non-empty.

\square

From the correctness of Brzozowski derivatives, this observation follows immediately.

Corollary 4

Let
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
be a regular expression over an alphabet
\Delta
and
D\subseteq\Delta
be the first letters in words in
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
, i.e.
D\coloneq\{a_{1}\mid a_{1}\ldots a_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})\}
.
Then, it holds that

\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})=\biguplus_{a\in D}\{a\cdot w\mid w\in\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}(\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}))\}\kern 5.0pt.

Intuitively, we pull out every first letter for union and concatenation to avoid the introduction of
\varepsilon
-transitions.
For this to work, we need to introduce a PSM derivative (function).
If we used the Brzozowski Derivative, we could not apply structural induction to prove equivalence of the regular expression and the PSM.
Still, we show sender-driven choice is preserved by the Brzozowski Derivative.

Lemma 29

Let
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
be an sender-driven RE
and
x\in\texttt{first}(\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}))
.
Then, it holds that
\operatorname{brz-deriv}(x,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is a sender-driven RE.

Proof

Brüggemann-Klein and
Wood [16]
show that deterministic REs, which they call
1-unambiguous
, are closed under the Brzozowski derivative.
Their result generalises to sender-driven REs.
They define star normal form for regular expressions
[
16
, Def. 3.3]
.
They recall that deterministic REs can always be specified by an RE in star normal form
[
15
]
.
With
[
16
, Thm. B]
, they show that the Brzozowski derivative of a deterministic RE in star normal form is again deterministic and in star normal form.
The conditions on sender-driven choice for REs do not restrict representability in star normal form and, thus, the result generalises to sender-driven REs.

\square

The last ingredient for our transformation is a procedure that applies the derivative to PSMs, preserving the properties of interest.

Lemma 30
(PSM for derivative)

Let
M=(Q,\Gamma,\delta,q_{0},F)
be a PSM that is
ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense, intermediate-recursion-free, and sink-final.
and let
a\in\texttt{first}(\mathcal{L}(M))
.
Then, there is a PSM,
\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,M)
, such that

\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,M^{\prime}))=\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,\mathcal{L}(M))
,
which is ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense, intermediate-recursion-free, and sink-final.
If
M
is sender-driven,
\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,M)
is sender-driven.

Proof

Let
q_{0}
be the initial state of
M
,

Q_{1}
be the states with incoming transitions from
q_{0}
,
and
Q_{2}
be the states with outgoing transition to
q_{0}
.
(Without loss of generality, we can assume that these are
\varepsilon
-transitions.)
Formally

Q_{1}\coloneq\{q_{1}\mid(q_{0},\_,q_{1})\in\delta\}
and

Q_{2}\coloneq\{q_{2}\mid(q_{2},\varepsilon,q_{0})\in\delta\}
.
By assumption that

a\in\texttt{first}(\mathcal{L}(M))
,
we know that there is
q_{1}\in Q_{1}
such that
(q_{0},a,q_{1})\in\delta
.
We construct
\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,M)
as follows.
We take
q_{1}
as its initial state and do only keep the states for which
q_{1}
is an ancestor.
For every state
q_{2}
in
Q_{2}
, which was not deleted, we copy the original PSM
M
, remove the state
q_{2}
and replace it by the initial state from the copy.
By assumption that the original PSM
M
is ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense, intermediate-recursion-free, and sink-final, this construction yields a PSM
\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,M)
with the same properties and
\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,M))=\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,\mathcal{L}(M))
.
By construction,
\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,M)
is sender-driven if
M
is sender-driven.

\square

With this, we can provide the procedure that translates REs to PSMs.

{procedure}

[RE to PSM]
Given a regular expression
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
without
\varepsilon
over
\Gamma
, we inductively construct the PSM
\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
:

1.

a
: one initial state with one transition labelled
a
to one final state;

2.

{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
:
We add one initial state.
We do the same for both
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
.
We describe it for
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
: we compute

\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}))

for every first letter
a

and add a transition labelled with the letter from the initial state to the initial state of the PSM.

3.

{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
:
We construct the FSM for
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
.
For
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
, we apply the derivative idea again:
we compute
\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))
for every first letter
a
and
copy each FSM as often as there are final states in the FSM for
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
and add transitions from each such final state.

4.

{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}^{*}
:
For Kleene Star, we construct the FSM for the inner regex and connect the final state(s) with the initial one by an
\varepsilon
-transition and make the initial one final.
(These are backward transitions and, thus, should to be labelled
\varepsilon
for the PSM to be dense.)

We prove the previous procedure to be correct.

Lemma 31

Let
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
be a regular expression over
\Gamma
without
\varepsilon
and
\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
be a PSM.
Then, the core language of both are the same:

\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})=\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}))
.
Also,

\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})

is
ancestor-recursive, non-merging, intermediate-recursion-free, dense, and sink-final.
If
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
is a sender-driven RE, then
\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt})
is a sender-driven PSM.

Proof

We prove the claims by induction on the structure of the regular expression
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}
.

The case for a single letter
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}=a
is obvious.

Let
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt}={r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
.
We first show that

\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})=\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))
.
The PSM construction applies the PSM derivative

\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))

for every
a\in\texttt{first}(\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))
and copies the resulting PSM for every final state of

\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1})
and adds a transition with label
a
.
Thus, for every word
w
in
\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})
,
we have that

•

w\in\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}))\cap\Gamma^{\omega}
or

•

w=u\cdot a\cdot v
with

u\in\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}))
,

a\in\texttt{first}(\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})))
,
and
v\in\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})))
.

By induction hypothesis, we have that

\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1})=\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}))

and

\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})=\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))
.
By
Lemma

30
,

\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))=\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))
.
Hence, we obtain:

•

w\in\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1})\cap\Gamma^{\omega}
or

•

w=u\cdot a\cdot v
with

u\in\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1})
,

a\in\texttt{first}(\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))
,
and
v\in\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{brz-deriv}(a,{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))
.

By the semantics of regular expressions and

Lemmas

27
and
28
,
it follows that

w\in\mathcal{L}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})
, which shows language equality.

By induction hypothesis, we know that

\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1})
and

\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})

are ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense, intermediate-recursion-free, and sink-final.
By
Lemma

30
,
for every

a\in\texttt{first}(\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})))
,
it holds that

\operatorname{psm-deriv}(a,\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}))

is
ancestor-recursive, non-merging, intermediate-recursion-free, dense, sink-final and sender-driven if
\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})
is.
Thus, by construction, the PSM

\operatorname{regex2psm}({r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}\cdot{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2})

is
ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense, intermediate-recursion-free, and sink-final,
where the multiple copies ensure ancestor-recursiveness and non-merging property and the derivatives preserve density;
and sender-driven choice is also preserved.

For
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}+{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
, the construction applies the PSM derivative to avoid introducing non-determinism (as is common in standard constructions for FSMs from REs) if it was not present before.
If it was there before, it preserves it to avoid subsequent merging and, thus, avoids introducing non-sink final states.
For sender-driven choice, the assumption for the regular expression yields that the first letters are pair-wise distinct and, thus, the newly introduced branching satisfies the sender-driven choice condition for PSMs.
The remaining reasoning is very similar to the previous case for concatenation and, hence, omitted.
Here, both
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
and
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt2}
are treated the same, like the second part of concatenation.

For
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}^{*}
, we simply introduce a backward transition, which ought to be labelled by
\varepsilon
and it is.
In fact, these are the only
\varepsilon
-transition, ensuring that the PSM is dense.
Note that we construct a PSM without forward transitions that are labelled with
\varepsilon
.
This is different from state machines for global types where every subterm of shape
\mu t\,.\,G
has only one incoming backward and one outgoing forward transition labelled by
\varepsilon
.
In this construction, we basically merge the states for
\mu t\,.\,G
and
G
.
It is also the place where recursion is introduced, ensuring ancestor-recursion and intermediate recursion freedom.
For sender-driven choice, analogously, the assumption for the regular expression yields that the first letters are pair-wise distinct and, thus, the branch that decides whether to start or repeat with
{r}_{\kern-0.40974pt1}
or continue with the next regular expression satisfies the sender-driven choice condition for the PSMs.

\square

Step
(3)
:
From Ancestor-recursive Non-merging Dense Intermediate-recursion-free PSMs to Global Types.

While the previous steps apply to arbitrary (sink-final) PSMs, this one only applies for Σ1-PSMs since global types specify send and receive events together.
This transformation is rather straightforward.
The global type can be constructed via a traversal of the
Σ1-PSM
.

{procedure}

[
Σ1-PSM
to Global Type]
Let
M
be
ancestor-recursive, dense, non-merging, and intermediate-recursion-free
Σ1-PSM
.
As a preprocessing step, we merge asynchronous events and assume
M
works on the alphabet of synchronous events
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
.
We start with an empty global type and start the traversal from initial state:

•

If the state is final: add
0
and return;

•

if the state has an incoming transition:

add
\mu t.
for fresh
t
and store
t
for this state;

•

if the state has an outgoing transition to previously seen state:

add
t
for the destination of the outgoing transition and return;

•

if the state has outgoing transitions to unseen states:

add
\Sigma_{i\in I}
with a fresh index set
I
(for
\lvert I\rvert
branches) with one branch for each next state with according transition label and recurse for each of next states.

Note that a state can have an incoming transition and more than one outgoing transitions, in contrast to the state machine of a global type where every subterm of shape
\mu t\,.\,G
has only one incoming and one outgoing transition labelled by
\varepsilon
.
In this construction, the states for
\mu t\,.\,G
and
G
are merged.
We denote the result of this procedure with
\operatorname{psm2gt}(M)
.

The following lemma states the correctness of the previous procedure.

Lemma 32

Let
M
be
ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense, intermediate-recursion-free, and sink-final
Σ1-PSM
and
\operatorname{psm2gt}(M)
be the global type constructed from
M
.
Then, their core languages are equal:

\mathcal{L}(M)=\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{psm2gt}(M))
.
If
M
is a sender-driven PSM, then
\operatorname{psm2gt}(M)
is a sender-driven global type.

Proof

The assumptions guarantee that the traversal does not revisit states and only sink states are final.
The preprocessing simplifies the translation to the corresponding terms of a global type.
The claim then follows easily by construction.
We sketch how to formalise it.
One can define a formalism that jointly/recursively represents languages starting from states in an FSM and (partial) global types.
The construction iteratively refines this representation,
preserving the specified language and sender-driven choice if given.

\square

Wrapping Up: From PSMs to Global Types.

Let us first observe that part of this workflow can be applied when using the more general alphabet
\Gamma
where send and receive events may not happen next to each other, yielding the following results.

Lemma 33

For every PSM
M
with
\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{S}(M)
, there is an
ancestor-recursive, non-merging, dense, and intermediate-recursion-free PSM
M^{\prime}
with the same core language.
If
M
is sink-final and satisfies mixed choice (sender-driven choice, or directed choice respectively),
then
M^{\prime}
is sink-final and satisfies mixed choice (sender-driven choice, or directed choice respectively).
If
M
is not sink-final, restrictions on choice are not preserved.

Proof

Let
M
be a sink-final PSM with
\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{S}(M)
.
We do a case analysis if
M
is sink-final.
If
M
is sink-final,

\operatorname{regex2psm}(\operatorname{psm2regex}(M))

is such a PSM and any restriction on choice is preserved by

Lemmas

26
and
31
.
If
M
is not sink-final,

\operatorname{regex2psm}(\operatorname{psm2regex}(\operatorname{psm2sink-final-psm}(M)))

is such a PSM by

Propositions

5
,
26
and
31
.

\square

For the special case of Σ1-PSMs, we can convert such PSMs to global types, proving the main result in this section.

See
6.1

Proof

For the first claim, let
M
be a sink-final
Σ1-PSM
.
We do a case analysis if
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{S}(M)
.

If so, we know that
\mathcal{S}(M)=\{\varepsilon\}
because
M
is sink-final and no transition is labelled by
\varepsilon
.
Then,
G=0
has the same core language.

If not, we can apply our workflow and construct the global type

\operatorname{psm2gt}(\operatorname{regex2psm}(\operatorname{psm2regex}(M)))

which represents the same core language and any restriction on choice is preserved by

Lemmas

26
,
31
and
32
.

With the optional step (0), our workflow can also transform non-sink-final Σ1-PSMs into global types; however, at the price of adding non-determinism.

Theorem 0.D.1

Every
Σ1-PSM

M
with
\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{S}(M)
can be represented as a non-deterministic global type with the same core language.

Proof

Let
M
be a non-sink-final
Σ1-PSM
with
\varepsilon\notin\mathcal{S}(M)
.
Then,

\operatorname{psm2gt}(\operatorname{regex2psm}(\operatorname{psm2regex}(\operatorname{psm2sink-final-psm}(M))))

is a non-deterministic global type that represents the same core language by

Propositions

5
,
26
,
31
and
32
.

\square

0.D.3
Additional Material for Consequence
(c)

Local types are defined analogously to global types.
We present local types which allow participants to send and receive from different participants, as defined in
[
60
]
.

Definition 41
(Local Types)

The
local types
for a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
are defined as:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\Coloneqq 0|\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.L_{i}|\ExtCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.L_{i}|μX.L|X

where the internal choice
(\IntCh)
and external choice
(\ExtCh)
both respect

∀i,j∈I.\;i≠j⇒({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i},m_{i})≠({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j},m_{j})
.
As for global types,
we assume every recursion variable is bound, each recursion
operator (
μ
) uses a different identifier
t
,
We assume
\lvert I\rvert>0
and, if
\lvert I\rvert=1
, we omit
\IntCh
and
\ExtCh
.
The semantics is defined analogously to global types, using a state machine
\mathsf{LAut}(\hbox{-})
, and is omitted.

The workflow of
Section

0.D.2
can obtain a local type from a sink-final FSM over
\Gamma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for a participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
If a deterministic FSM is not sink-final, there is no sink-final deterministic FSM for the same language, making a non-deterministic local type the most one can achieve.
If the FSM has no mixed-choice states, the transformation yields a local type.
If it has, the structure still resembles the one of local types but requires the simultaneous specification of receiving and sending.

See
6.2

0.D.4
Additional Material for
(d)

In this section, we provide the formalisation to prove
Theorem

6.3
.

When using local types, final configurations are always sink-state configurations, i.e. where each participant is in a sink state.
For our setting, this is not the case and has repercussions on the semantics and meaning of deadlocks:
we can have final configurations where some participants are not in a final state.
If there is no next transition for such a configuration,
we call it a soft deadlock.
We define the notion of soft deadlocks and recall the definition of strong projectability.

Definition 42
(Soft Deadlocks, Strong Projectability)

Let
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a CSM.
A configuration
(\vec{q},\xi)
is a
soft deadlock

if there is no
(\vec{q^{\prime}}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q^{\prime}},\xi^{\prime})
with

(\vec{q},\xi)\to(\vec{q^{\prime}}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{q^{\prime}},\xi^{\prime})

and

(\vec{q},\xi)
is no final sink-state configuration.
We say
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is free from soft deadlocks if every reachable configuration is no soft deadlock.
A language
L\subseteq\Gamma^{\omega}
is said to be
strongly projectable
if there exists a CSM
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that

\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is free from soft deadlocks

(soft deadlock freedom)
,
and

L
is the language of
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

(protocol fidelity)
.
We say that
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a strong projection of
L
.

With this notion of deadlock, intuitively, a configuration can only be considered actually final if no state machine has an outgoing transition.
It is obvious that every deadlock is a soft deadlock.
Different applications call for different notions of deadlock freedom.
In distributed computing, it is fine if a server keeps listening for incoming requests while, in embedded computing, it can be essential that all participants eventually stop.
We believe this is a design choice.

Intuitively, if one aims for soft deadlock freedom, no state with outgoing transitions needs to be final because,
for soft deadlocks, only final sink-state configurations matter.
One could require a projection to be sink-final.
However, while this is a sufficient, it is not obviously necessary:
any final non-sink-state configuration could simply not get stuck, never exploiting the fact it is final.
We show that this is not the case if one of two conditions hold for CSMs.

We use the subset construction from
[
57
, Def. 5.4]
, denoted by
\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
for participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, which simply projects the global type’s state machine onto the participant alphabet and determinises the result.

Definition 43
(Subset construction)

Let
G
be a global type and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a participant.
Then, the
subset construction
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is defined as

\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=\bigl(Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},F_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\bigr)\text{ where }

•

\delta(s,a)\coloneq\{q^{\prime}\in Q_{G}\mid\exists q\in s,q\xrightarrow{a}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}q^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}

for
every

s\subseteq Q_{G}
and

a\in\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,

•

s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\{q\in Q_{G}\mid q_{0,G}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}q\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}
,

•

Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\mathrm{lfp}_{\{s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}}^{\subseteq}\lambda Q.\,Q\cup\{\delta(s,a)\mid s\in Q\land a\in\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
,

•

\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq{\delta}|_{Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\times\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, and

•

F_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\{s\in Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mid s\cap F_{G}\neq\emptyset\}
.

To prove
Theorem

6.3
,
we add two more equivalent statements and prove four implications, yielding a cycle and equivalence of all statements.
It is trivial that

Theorem

6.3

follows from

Theorem

0.D.2
.

Theorem 0.D.2

Let
G
be a projectable global type.
Then, the following statements are equivalent, where
\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
is the subset construction of
G
onto
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
:

(i)

There is a sink-final CSM that is a projection of
G
and satisfies feasible eventual reception or every of its state machines is deterministic.

G
can be implemented by a sink-final CSM that satisfies feasible eventual reception or every of its state machines is deterministic.

(ii)

The subset construction
\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
is sink-final for every participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

(iii)

All reachable final configurations of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are sink-state.

(iv)

There is a CSM that is a strong projection of
G
and this CSM satisfies feasible eventual reception or every of its state machines is deterministic.

Proof

Note that the subset projection is basically the subset construction but checks validity conditions
[
57
, Sec. 5]
.
Together with completeness of their approach
[
57
, Thm. 7.1]
, the Send Validity condition enforces that any final state in the subset projection, and thus construction of a projectable global type, cannot have outgoing send transitions.

Proof that

(i)

implies

(ii)
:

Let
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a sink-final CSM that satisfies feasible eventual reception or every of its state machines is deterministic, and is a projection of
G
.
Towards a contradiction, assume that the subset construction is not sink-final.
Without loss of generality, let
\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
be the subset construction with at least one final non-sink state and let
s
be one of the states that is final and has outgoing transitions.

By the fact that
s
is final, there is
0\in s
.
By the fact that
s
has outgoing transitions, there is
G^{\prime}\in s
with
G^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}G^{\prime\prime}
for
x{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\in\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
and some
G^{\prime\prime}
.
Because of Send Validity
[
57
, Def. 5.4]
, we have
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm
for some participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and message
m
.

In the subset construction, two subterms
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
do only occur in the same state
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
of
\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
if there are two runs

G\xrightarrow{w}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G_{1}

and

G\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G_{2}

with

w\in\Gamma^{*}
,

w^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{*}
,
and
w{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
Here, we use choose
G_{1}=0
and
G_{2}=G^{\prime}
.
Thus, we have
w\in\mathcal{L}(G)
.

Let

(\vec{s},\xi)

be the configuration of the
subset construction
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

that is reached after processing
w
.
Because of protocol fidelity, determinacy of the subset construction and
w\in\mathcal{L}(G)
, it holds that
(\vec{s},\xi)
is final.
Recall there is a transition from
G^{\prime}
to
G^{\prime\prime}
labelled with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{\to}\kern 0.39993pt{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\kern 0.39993pt{:}\kern 0.39993ptm
, so we can extend
w^{\prime}
to obtain
w^{\prime\prime}\coloneq w^{\prime}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\triangleright}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m

for which

w{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=w^{\prime\prime}{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
Let

(\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})

be the configuration of

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

after processing
w^{\prime\prime}
.
By construction of the traces, the channels are empty after processing
w^{\prime\prime}
.
Hence, we have
\xi^{\prime\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=m
with the additional send event in
w^{\prime\prime}
.
Because of

w{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=w^{\prime\prime}{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
, it holds that

\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let us consider the two configurations of

\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

that are reached with
w
and
w^{\prime\prime}
.
By

[
60
, Lm. 20]
,
they will have the same channel contents as the subset construction respectively.
Let

(\vec{t},\xi)

and

(\vec{t}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{t}^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})

be the configurations of

\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

after processing
w
and
w^{\prime\prime}
respectively.
By

w{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}=w^{\prime\prime}{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
,
it is possible and we assume that

\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\vec{t}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{t}^{\prime\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Note that we do not assume determinacy of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
but we can assume that both runs end in the same state for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
since all ways of non-determinism need to be accounted for.
We show that
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is not sink-final: it is final but has at least one outgoing transition.

To start, we show that
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is final.
It suffices to show that

(\vec{t},\xi)

is a final configuration.
By the fact that

(\vec{s},\xi)

is final,
\xi
has only empty channels.
Towards a contradiction, assume that
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is not final for some participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
Then,
w
is not in
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
if there is no other run for
w
but, if there were,
\{\!\!\{A^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
still deadlocks in the configuration
(\vec{t},\xi)
, contradicting deadlock freedom.
Hence,

(\vec{t},\xi)
is final.

It remains to show that
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has an outgoing transition.
We do a case analysis on the side condition for

\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

First, we assume that
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
satisfies feasible eventual reception.
We use the second configuration

(\vec{t}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{t}^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})

where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is in the same state.
We know that
m
is the first message in
\xi^{\prime\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
It was sent and must be received.
Thus,
\vec{t}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{t}^{\prime\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has at least one outgoing transition.

Second, assume that
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is deterministic for every participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
Again, we use the second configuration

(\vec{t}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{t}^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})

where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is in the same state.
By the semantics of global types, there is an extension
w^{\prime\prime\prime}
of
w^{\prime\prime}
with

w^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(G)
that contains the receive event
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mathbin{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\triangleleft}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m
for the enqueued message
m
.
If
w^{\prime\prime\prime}
is finite, it is straightforward that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
needs to be able to receive the message
m
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
to satisfy protocol fidelity, ensuring an outgoing transition.
If
w^{\prime\prime}
is infinite, towards a contradiction, assume that
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has no outgoing transition.
The semantics require that every prefix
u\in\operatorname{pref}(w^{\prime\prime\prime})
is in
\operatorname{pref}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.
However, if
\vec{t}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
could not receive
m
, because of determinacy of
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, there is a prefix of
w^{\prime\prime\prime}
that is not.
This contradicts the assumption that
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a projection of
G
.

Proof that

(ii)

implies

(iii)
:

By assumption, all final configurations are sink-state configurations.
Hence, all reachable final configurations are sink-state configurations.

Proof that

(iii)

implies

(iv)
:

We claim that

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

is such a CSM.
Because of soundness
[
57
, Thm. 6.1]
,
we know that

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

satisfies protocol fidelity and is deadlock-free.
Hence, it suffices to show

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

is free from soft deadlocks.
Since

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

is deadlock-free,
the only way there could be soft deadlocks is that there is a reachable stuck final configuration that is no sink-state configuration.
This is impossible because every reachable final configuration is a sink-state configuration by assumption.
It remains to show that

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(G,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

satisfies one of both side conditions.
Both properties hold.
First, the subset construction determinises by definition.
Second, every global type satisfies feasible eventual reception by construction and a projection preserves this property.

Proof that

(iv)

implies

(i)
:

We know there is a strong projection for
G
(with certain properties) and want to show there is a sink-final projection (with the same properties).
From the assumption, let
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a strong projection of
G
.
By definition, every strong projection is also a projection of
G
because soft deadlock freedom implies deadlock freedom.
We assume it is not sink-final for some participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
as the claim follows trivially otherwise.
This means that
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has final states that have outgoing transitions.
We will show that these states do not need to be final for any such
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Hence, we can turn
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
to a sink-final CSM
\{\!\!\{A^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
by inductively applying this to all participants whose state machines are not sink-final.

We claim that, for a strong projection
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, whether a state is final or not does only matter for soft deadlock freedom and not protocol fidelity.
Assume it was relevant for protocol fidelity.
Then, there is a reachable non-sink-state configuration that is final, which means that all participants are in final states and the channels are empty.
We established earlier that no final state can have an outgoing send transition.
Thus, this would constitute a soft deadlock, contradicting the fact that
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a strong projection.

From soft deadlock freedom, we know that there is no stuck reachable final non-sink-state configuration.
In other words, all the stuck configurations are final sink-state configurations.
Thus, any non-sink final state does not need to be final.
This proves the claim that the existence of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
always implies the existence of a sink-final CSM
\{\!\!\{A^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
which is our witness for projectability.
The side conditions for both statements are the same and not affected by our construction so they simply carry over.

\square

For our result, it is fine to assume projectability of
G
as it is a prerequisite for strong projectability.
If we aim for a strong projection, we can construct the global type’s subset projection (the subset construction with validity conditions) and check if it is sink-final.
If it provides one that is not, there is no strong projection of it.
If this is undesirable, the protocol needs redesigning.
To obtain local types from a CSM of sink-final state machines, we can use
Theorem

6.2
. This requires no mixed-choice states, which is always the case for the subset projection.

Corollary 5

Let
G
be a strongly projectable global type.
Then, there is a local type
L_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
such that

\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(L_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a strong projection of
G
.

0.D.5
Projectability and Strong Projectability

for Global Types with Mixed Choice is Undecidable

In
Section

4.2
, we constructed a sink-final mixed-choice
Σ1-PSM
to prove undecidability of the respective projectability problem.
With our results from

Section

0.D.2
,
we can transform this encoding into a mixed-choice global type.

See
2

Proof

From
Theorem

4.2
, we know that the projectability problem is undecidable for sink-final mixed-choice Σ1-PSMs in general.
From
Theorem

6.1
, we know that such PSMs can be transformed into a mixed-choice global type, which proves the claim for the projectability problem.
By construction, all participants are informed when the protocol of the encoding ends, making the projectability and soft projectability problem equivalent.
This proves the claim for the soft projectability problem.

\square
